Abstract: Dense collaborative mapping is essential for search and rescue, building inspection, and warehouse automation, where panoramic cameras capture the entire surroundings in a single frame. Unlike pinhole agents that share views only at similar headings, panoramic agents observe overlapping scenes at any 3D orientation, yet searching full rotations also produces mutually consistent false loop closures across distinct symmetric rooms. Moreover, continuous monocular scale drift splits rigidly merged maps at revisited surfaces, while depth noise at short baselines distorts loop translation directions. To address these challenges, we propose CoSphere-SLAM, the first multi-agent Gaussian splatting SLAM framework for panoramic cameras, whose spherical loop closure aligns spherical features over $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ via fast Fourier correlation across arbitrary orientations and rejects symmetric false closures through global $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ consensus. Building on these closures, directional global optimization solves a per-keyframe $\mathrm{Sim}(3)$ pose graph with log-scale regularization to correct scale drift while separating optical-flow translation directions from depth-based magnitudes. Finally, omnidirectional Gaussian fusion transforms each Gaussian with its keyframe similarity and renders Gaussians at their maximum ray response to jointly refine poses and maps. Experiments on indoor and cross-session multi-agent panoramic benchmarks demonstrate that CoSphere-SLAM achieves state-of-the-art trajectory accuracy and rendering quality among single-agent and multi-agent methods. Project website: https://cosphere-slam.github.io.

</details>

Abstract: Scenes captured with handheld and wearable cameras are still shipped to a CUDA workstation for 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) training. Training on the device that captured them, or on its companion phone or laptop, requires more than translating kernels: GPUs and drivers differ in parallel features, memory limits, and command submission, and these differences decide whether a training schedule executes and returns the correct update. We present Cross3DGS, a cross-vendor, cross-platform Vulkan trainer that keeps one optimizer and chooses its execution schedule per device in two steps: rules on device limits, workload size, and observed driver failures exclude the schedules known not to run correctly, and a fixed preference order selects one of the remaining feasible schedules. The same core completes standard-quality training (30k updates, one million Gaussians) on every configuration we measured, spanning NVIDIA, Intel, Arm, Qualcomm, AMD, and Apple GPUs and including Android devices and an iPhone, whereas the existing portable trainers VkSplat and Brush do not run on some of the mobile GPUs. Averaged over 11 scenes on a workstation, Cross3DGS has higher quality than VkSplat with no significant difference in training time, and Brush reports about three times the training time at comparable quality. The final quality of Cross3DGS is nearly identical across devices. A mobile application turns a handheld capture into a viewable model entirely on the device.

</details>

Abstract: Reconstructing an articulated object with its 3D movable parts, joint parameters and unseen articulation states from an uncalibrated monocular video is essential for robot manipulation and interactive digital twins. Existing per-scene optimisation methods require minutes to hours and external depth or camera poses, whereas feed-forward 3D foundation models reconstruct dense point maps in one pass without segmenting movable parts or inferring joints. Even on these monocular point maps, regressing or fitting continuous joints directly fails on casual videos, since cross-frame attention blurs moving part boundaries in deep layers, underestimates motion along the viewing ray and leaves continuous axes ill-conditioned under small opening angles. To address these challenges, we propose PhyVGGT, a single-pass framework on a frozen 3D foundation model that anchors moving-part matching and closed-form joint estimation to the undistorted geometry of the stationary base. First, our Part and Motion Decoder fuses intermediate per-frame tokens with global tokens and references part queries to a static-base query to segment 3D parts, align point maps on the static base and predict learned Bingham priors. Second, our Kinematic Inference Layer fits an orthogonal Manhattan frame to static surface normals, selects the joint axis in closed form under a Bingham posterior combining pairwise motion scatter with the learned priors, and solves a linear system for the hinge pivot to drive unseen states. Trained solely on synthetic data, PhyVGGT achieves state-of-the-art monocular performance across five benchmarks, outperforming FAMOS and monocular ArtiPoint, surpassing posed RGB-D ArtiPoint in median error, and running in 6.8 s per 48-frame video against hours of per-scene optimisation in Articulat3D. Project page: https://phyvggt.github.io.

</details>

Abstract: Camouflaged vehicle detection is challenging because camouflage suppresses discriminative cues across sensing modalities. Existing multimodal methods often require precise pixel-level registration and feature-level fusion, creating a trade-off between accuracy and latency. We propose a spectral-polarization-infrared tri-modal detection framework co-designed with a multi-aperture imaging setup to exploit complementary imaging physics. A consensus-guided decision-level fusion strategy combines cross-modal detections within bounded registration tolerances, reducing dependence on precise alignment while maintaining accuracy and real-time performance. We construct a tri-modal camouflaged vehicle dataset containing infrared, polarization, and spectral data. Experiments show that the proposed method achieves 82.7% mean Average Precision (mAP) with an end-to-end latency of 16 ms, outperforming representative feature-level fusion approaches. On the public MID-1K dataset, the framework generalizes to RGB-Depth-Infrared modalities without architectural modification and improves mAP@0.5 by 5.7% over state-of-the-art methods. These results demonstrate the potential of decision-level consensus fusion and multi-aperture sensing for robust and deployable camouflaged vehicle detection.

</details>

Abstract: Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) foundation models provide transferable representations for diverse perception tasks in all-weather Earth observation. However, their development often relies on costly large-scale SAR pretraining, raising the question of whether such extensive domain-specific pretraining is necessary. We find that modern multimodal large language models (MLLMs) already provide visual encoders that can outperform dedicated SAR foundation models as downstream initializations. This motivates a new paradigm: reuse general visual capabilities and devote domain training to brief specialization. We therefore introduce SARVIA, an adaptive post-pretraining method that specializes modern MLLMs using self-supervised visual questions with computable answers. These questions are constructed from unlabeled observations through general image tasks that capture spatial relations and SAR-specific tasks that exploit native measurements and SAR--optical correspondence. A shared answer-prediction objective jointly trains low-rank adapters across visual and language components, with loss-driven task sampling. We then export the adapted visual encoder as a standalone backbone. With 600 adaptation steps and 2 GPU-hours, SARVIA outperforms the compared foundation models on all six SAR detection and segmentation benchmarks. Compared with dedicated SAR pretraining, SARVIA reduces domain-training costs by orders of magnitude while improving visual transfer across architectures and scales. This new paradigm shifts the emphasis from repeated, costly pretraining to a reusable specialization process that can benefit from advances in open MLLMs. Dataset and code: https://github.com/zcablii/SARVIA.

</details>

Abstract: Latent diffusion models rely on a decoder to map denoised latents to pixels, adding complexity and inference cost. We introduce Decoding Without a Decoder (DWD), which lets a finetuned diffusion transformer produce final pixels directly through a linear head. Unlike previous methods, where the denoising output leads to final pixels, DWD separates the two roles: the original denoising head continues to drive sampling, then the new DWD head predicts the pixels at the final step. We extend pretrained latent models with DWD efficiently by finetuning only the last few transformer blocks, training the DWD head with pixel quality losses while anchoring the denoising head to the pretrained model to preserve the sample trajectory. Across latent image and video generators, DWD removes the decoder and reduces inference cost while maintaining or even improving pixel quality. On Wan video generation, it visibly reduces detail distortion and flickering, lowering warping error by 23%. Remarkably, the same separation also improves pixel diffusion models that do not even use a decoder: a final-step DWD head produces substantially better texture detail in text-to-image generation, and improves ImageNet FID from 1.55 to 1.43, a new state of the art among pixel flow models.

</details>

Abstract: Video understanding with multiple multimodal language models requires a communication interface that can transfer complementary visual observations. Text-based communication is interpretable, but introduces an intermediate generation stage and can omit information relevant to subsequent reasoning. We study Cross-Modal StateKV Injection, a provider--carrier framework that communicates through key--value (KV) states at visual-token positions. Provider models observe complementary video frames; their visual KV states are aligned and projected into the carrier representation space and integrated through gated residual updates. Injection preserves the carrier KV-cache sequence length, while retaining the additional computation required by the providers and the communication module. The reported experiments on MLVU and Video-MME compare carrier-only inference, text-based communication, and StateKV with two provider architectures. Results show modest, task-dependent accuracy gains and lower reported latency than the evaluated text-communication baseline, with some categories remaining unchanged or decreasing. Increasing provider-side frame coverage offers additional gains on selected tasks without extending the carrier cache. These findings motivate further study of visual KV communication under explicitly controlled observation and computation budgets.

</details>


### [164] [Inertial Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22314)

Abstract: The physical world has inertia: it evolves from what already exists rather than being recreated at every instant. However, many existing video world models lack an explicit mechanism for inertial state evolution, leaving the continuity of motion and structure implicit in video prediction. During autoregressive rollouts, repeated reconstruction can accumulate errors and destabilize the predicted dynamics. To address this limitation, we introduce InertialWorld, an Inertial Video World Model that makes inertia a structural prior for autoregressive prediction: existing scene evidence is carried across time rather than reconstructed at every step. Central to this formulation is Inertial Memory, which turns memory from passive predictive context into an evolving video state with two complementary views: a dense Eulerian grid preserves scene-aligned evidence, while persistent Lagrangian carriers bind local content to learned canonical coordinates. At each step, the model transports the carrier content according to action-conditioned motion, predicts the complete next video state from the transported carriers and dense grid, and uses a learned gate to combine transported and newly predicted content into the memory for the next step. Extensive experiments across multiple domains, including robot manipulation, 3D game simulation, and open-world navigation, demonstrate that InertialWorld achieves competitive autoregressive prediction performance against strong video world model baselines. Codes link: https://github.com/YuanGao-YG/InertialWorld

</details>


### [165] [SIDUMN: Self-Supervised Image Denoising Under Symmetric Multiplicative Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22317)

Abstract: Self-supervised denoising is challenging when the corruption is multiplicative and signal-dependent. In this letter, we propose SIDUMN, a symmetric noisier framework for self-supervised denoising under mean-one symmetric multiplicative noise. Given noisy observations and the noise law, the method samples an independent synthetic noise factor from the same law and constructs two complementary noisier images by applying the factor and its reflection about one. The denoising network is trained to predict one noisier image from the other, and at inference the reconstructions of $K$ such antithetic pairs are averaged. We show that the population target of the training loss is the clean conditional mean minus a multiplicative cross-term. We further prove that, for any trained network, antithetic averaging never increases the expected squared error of a single noisier-input reconstruction. Numerical results validate the theoretical findings and the proposed method.

</details>

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) models often produce high-quality images that follow familiar visual conventions, even in response to open-ended prompts such as ``a creative [CLS]''. Existing methods encourage novelty by exploring semantic conditions or modifying features and guidance during denoising. Pretrained weights offer a complementary source of creative variation, but unstructured perturbations can have little visual effect or degrade image quality. We introduce Coupled Spectral Perturbation (CosPert), a training-free method that targets attention projections in layers identified by a one-time sensitivity analysis. For each projection, CosPert uses a shared random transformation and its transpose to couple updates to the singular-vector factors while perturbing the spectral gains. A common relative budget calibrates the magnitude of weight changes across projections, and a sigmoid schedule concentrates their influence in early denoising and gradually attenuates them during refinement. CosPert provides plug-and-play creativity enhancement at inference time. Once the perturbations are prepared, sampling requires no auxiliary networks or additional model evaluations. On both FLUX.1 and Stable Diffusion 3.5, CosPert enhances visual creativity, producing more varied object structures while preserving category identity. Across 50 object categories, CosPert achieves the highest mean CREward among the evaluated methods while maintaining strong prompt alignment.

</details>

Abstract: A visual question answering (VQA) model can pass every image-only and question-only test yet fail when the same changes occur together. Standard robustness reports average over conditions and therefore cannot say which accepted requests remain vulnerable. We introduce M3-Test, a coverage audit that aligns each clean input with its single-modality variants and a complete 5x5 family, 4x4 strength joint library, so that any reduced test suite can be replayed on the same requests. Across 2.6 million responses from four vision-language models, joint failures affect 26-29% of the Qwen questions that pass all 40 single-modality tests, although fewer than 0.6% of their joint records fail. Passing the 25 maximum-strength corners does not close this gap: 11-14% of the accepted questions still fail elsewhere in the library. Re-executing the archived failures reproduces about 90% of them, against a 0.3-0.6% flip rate among matched passing controls, and re-checking the most fragile failure records supports 80-89% of them. On the evidence examined, neither execution noise nor grading error can fully account for the gap. The same aligned grids make previous evaluations reusable. On held-out images, a risk order learned from other models discovers about 80% of affected Qwen questions within 25 additional queries, compared with 65-68% for maximum-first and 53-57% for pairwise covering arrays. M3-Test thus turns a passing robustness suite into a request-level account of what remains untested, supporting acceptance, regression review and test allocation.

</details>


### [168] [DAP-MR: Degradation-Aware Prompt-Magnitude Routing for Real-World Ultrasound Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22331)
*Ngoc-Hoang-Lam Le,Thi-An Le,Dinh-Hung Le,Manh-Hung Nguyen,Kai-Lung Hua,Ching-Chun Huang*

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: When super-resolution models designed for natural images are used on ultrasound scans, relying on semantic text prompts can lead to unreliable results. The tags these models generate often do not match the actual restoration needs, and specialized medical terms may be misunderstood by general-purpose text encoders. Instead, prompts focused on common image degradations are more directly useful for guiding restoration. However, using a single prompt to describe both the type and severity of image degradation can entangle these factors, making it hard for the model to know how strong the degradation is. To address this limitation, we introduce Degradation-Aware Prompt--Magnitude Routing (DAP-MR). Each degradation type is represented by a dedicated token initialized from the contextualized EOS embedding of a degradation-oriented prompt and subsequently adapted during ultrasound SR training. A separate magnitude router predicts a continuous, input-dependent magnitude that modulates the corresponding token. Thus, language provides a degradation-aware initialization for what should be restored, while magnitude routing supplies an explicit signal for how strongly it should influence restoration. DAP-MR can be added to both one-step and multi-step diffusion super-resolution frameworks without changing their core structures. Our experiments--on synthetic datasets with both in-domain and under anatomical-domain shift, as well as unpaired real-world ultrasound data--show that DAP-MR delivers better restoration quality and generalizes more consistently across different domains.

</details>


### [169] [PURE: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Multi-Granularity Multimodal Identity Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22343)

Abstract: Identity matching asks whether two records denote the same identity, yet the answer depends on the target granularity. Two records may match at the base level but not at the variant level: they share an underlying design while differing in attributes such as color or size. Final matching accuracy does not separately characterize the capabilities involved. To study this problem, we use e-commerce as a challenging real-world domain. We introduce PURE, a diagnostic benchmark for multi-granularity multimodal identity matching covering four capability dimensions: Perception, Understanding, Reasoning, and Evidence-grounded Judgment. The benchmark comprises 1,457 multiple-choice questions across 14 tasks, derived from real-world matching pairs and verified by two domain experts. Across ten recent MLLMs, overall scores range from 47.1% to 86.0%. Understanding receives the highest mean score and Judgment the lowest, with weaknesses in visual difference localization and variant-level rule judgment. Modality ablations show that models benefit from multimodal evidence but can incorrectly identify conflicts between matching records. In direct matching, most models recognize matching pairs more reliably than non-matching pairs at the base level, whereas the reverse holds at the variant level. PURE therefore reveals what aggregate matching accuracy hides: not only whether models judge a pair correctly, but where they struggle to interpret evidence and apply matching criteria.

</details>


### [170] [SignAligner: Dual-Granularity Span Generation for Sign Language Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22409)

Abstract: Large sign-language corpora benefit from temporal labels at two scales: subtitle spans for subtitle alignment and word spans for sign spotting. Sign spottings already locate words in time at corpus scale, but training subtitle alignment jointly on subtitle and word spans remains underexplored. We present SignAligner, a prompt-conditioned visual-language span generator that maps a sign-language video and a subtitle-sentence or target-word query to a textual start/end frame interval. It is first trained jointly on subtitle spans and word spans from sign spottings, then refined with overlap-based rewards while retaining auxiliary word examples. On the subtitle test set of the British Broadcasting Corporation-Oxford British Sign Language (BOBSL) corpus, the refined model reaches 66.68 F1@0.50 (F1 with a temporal intersection-over-union threshold of one half), the best among compared systems without external sign-video resources. This overlap-reward refinement outperforms cross-entropy continuation from the same jointly trained model. Joint training with word spans improves subtitle alignment in a pair of our generators matched in code, labels and maximum update budget. The advantage remains after both models of the pair are refined with overlap rewards. For word queries, the refined model follows the queried word, not a fixed position in the video: when the word's sign lies away from the video centre, it is far more accurate than predictions that use position alone. Together, these findings support one shared span generator refined with overlap rewards, aligning subtitles and producing word spans for human review.

</details>


### [171] [SandBAR: A Band-Asymmetric Architecture for Underwater Image Restoration with Removable Frequency Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22355)
*Yanzi Liu,Haoran Hou,Jiachi Tong,Shen Chen,Shuang Chen*

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Light scattering, absorption and turbidity degrade underwater imagery through complementary frequency tendencies: large-scale, channel-dependent appearance distortions and local high-frequency detail loss. This band asymmetry motivates different forms of restoration for global colour and local structure. Our primary contribution is SandBAR, a novel unified band-asymmetric architecture for underwater image restoration. Rather than treating frequency processing as an isolated module, SandBAR organises the restoration pipeline around band asymmetry at the block, encoder and objective levels. Within this architecture, we introduce a novel band-asymmetric sandwich block with a reserved post-attention repair slot and the Split-band Parallel Asymmetric Repair Kernel (SPARK), a novel module that applies channel-aware large-support correction to the low band, which predominantly carries large-scale appearance distortions, and lightweight local refinement to the detail-bearing high band, thereby restoring global colour while preserving fine structure. To deliver the global cast to the depth at which the network represents global structure, a learnable low-frequency guidance path injects a low-pass view of the input into the deep encoder, and a three-scale spatial-frequency objective supervises the same axis throughout the decoder. Building on the reserved post-attention slot, we further introduce Post-attention Scaffold Training (PaST), a novel removable nonlinear scaffolding scheme that trains the permanent SandBAR graph under band-specific capacity and runs it at inference without that capacity. SandBAR reaches 29.89 / 28.92 / 22.77 dB on EUVP / LSUI / UIEB and establishes a new three-domain mean state of the art on the benchmark family, exceeding the strongest published result by approximately 1.0 dB. PaST adds no parameters or FLOPs at inference and preserves matched latency.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved impressive performance on a wide range of vision-language tasks. However, it remains unclear whether their predictions are grounded in the visual content itself or are largely driven by statistical co-occurrence patterns and commonsense correlations learned from large-scale image-text corpora. Existing vision-language benchmarks predominantly consist of samples that conform to human commonsense, allowing models to achieve strong performance by exploiting dataset priors rather than genuinely understanding image content. To address this limitation, we present ContraSense, a systematically constructed benchmark for evaluating faithful vision-language understanding. ContraSense consists of carefully curated counter-intuitive image-text pairs that deliberately violate common expectations, substantially reducing the effectiveness of statistical biases and requiring models to rely on visual evidence for reasoning. We conduct comprehensive evaluations on a diverse set of representative open- and closed-source MLLMs. The results reveal a substantial performance gap between current MLLMs and humans: even the strongest closed-source model achieves only about 60\% accuracy, compared with 96.04\% for human participants. These findings demonstrate that current MLLMs still struggle with faithful visual understanding and highlight the need for more robust multimodal reasoning grounded in image content. The ContraSense benchmark is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/LingLin00/ContraSense.

</details>


### [173] [CoBoVLA: Decoupling Language from Action with Discrete Codes for Robust and Interpretable Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22395)

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models achieve high success rates on the LIBERO benchmark but remain sensitive to paraphrasing. Existing VLAs ranging from 0.6B to 7.5B parameters experience a 22-52 point performance drop on LIBERO-Para while SmolVLA exhibits an even larger decline, from 83\% to 8\%. Because most VLAs jointly learn language understanding and action generation, it is difficult to determine whether a failure originates from misunderstanding an instruction or executing it incorrectly. To address these challenges, we introduce CoBoVLA, or CodeBookVLA, a 250M-parameter VLA that decouples language understanding, planning, and control through learned discrete interpretable codes. A text encoder maps the instructions to shared step codes, which a visual planner then converts into action code sequences executed by a language-independent actor. This design improves robustness to instruction paraphrasing while allowing failures to be traced to individual stages. It also enables efficient real-time control on resource-constrained hardware by encoding instructions only once and using compact discrete representations to keep each component lightweight. CoBoVLA achieves 91.4\% success on LIBERO-Goal, compared with 82.8\% for SmolVLA, under a matched protocol, with 17 ms per control step latency. On LIBERO-Para, it achieves 81.1\% across 4,092 unseen paraphrases and 78.5\% on matched episodes, compared with 36.5\% for SmolVLA LoRA fine-tuned on paraphrases. Without two-step demonstrations, CoBoVLA completes 48.8\% of 160 free-form two-step tasks. Finally, success reaches 91\% when instructions are correctly encoded but falls to 27.2\% otherwise, identifying language understanding as the main source of remaining failures. Together, these results demonstrate that decoupling language understanding and control can improve instruction robustness while making VLA failures easier to diagnose.

</details>

Abstract: We propose a self-configuring framework that extends the nnU-Net workflow to both segmentation and classification in 3D medical imaging. Building on nnU-Net's self-configuring method, the framework reuses representations learned by a segmentation model for image-level classification. Its encoder features and predicted segmentation probabilities are used by a lightweight classifier. Two information-sharing strategies are investigated. In the frozen-backbone branch, features are augmented with region-of-interest information, predicted foreground volume, and segmentation confidence. In the adaptation branch, the final encoder stage is partially fine-tuned for classification, with an auxiliary segmentation loss to preserve segmentation-related information. Additional five-fold experiments showed that more complex configurations did not consistently improve classification performance. On the public validation leaderboard, our selected framework achieved an Overall score of 0.8056. In the official FLARE 2026 Task 2 final evaluation, our framework ranked first overall, achieving the highest DSC, NSD, and AUROC across all four testing tasks, including two hidden testing tasks. Local inference measurements also remained below the challenge limits of $60 \mathrm{s}$ per case and $4 \mathrm{GB}$ GPU memory. These results demonstrate the practical value of reusing segmentation representations for classification while maintaining a simple and efficient framework.

</details>

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) jointly generate future video and robot actions through iterative denoising. Few-step distillation reduces the number of model evaluations, but its coarse updates can move the student away from the teacher's trajectory. We propose Anchored Path Matching (APM), which supervises endpoint and short-transition predictions from states reached by the student. From each state, we continue the teacher to zero noise and use its endpoint and first intermediate state as targets. Modality-routed adapters learn to match both targets from the same starting state. When the two gradients conflict, anchor-priority updates preserve the endpoint gradient and remove the opposing component of the local gradient within each modality. One checkpoint supports 1-, 2-, and 4-step joint generation. The sampling budget can be chosen at deployment without retraining or teacher queries. On RoboTwin 2.0, we train only on Clean demonstrations and evaluate directly on the domain-randomized Random setting. At one step, APM improves closed-loop success over continued base distillation at matched inference budgets by 2.0 percentage points on Clean and 1.4 on Random. Video peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) rises from 30.169 to 32.317 dB, with gains in structural similarity (SSIM) and learned perceptual image patch similarity (LPIPS). On two real-robot tasks, APM improves success over the one-step teacher and reduces control-cycle latency by $21.3\times$ relative to the eight-step teacher.Project page: https://jialonglong.github.io/Anchored-Path-Matching/.

</details>

Abstract: Spatial reasoning is a fundamental component of world understanding for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While existing research has advanced the perception of spatial relations in static scenes, the domain of dynamic spatial simulation remains relatively unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce MentalGrid, a benchmark focused on spatial reasoning tasks that require models to simulate evolving spatial states without intermediate visual feedback. Extensive evaluation on our benchmark reveals a significant deficiency in the spatial simulation capabilities of existing models. Specifically, we find that performance is constrained by perceptual limitations in simple simulation scenarios and hindered by inadequate multi-step reasoning as complexity increases. To address these challenges, we propose a three-stage curriculum learning framework utilizing 16k specialized spatial reasoning samples. This approach yields SpatialWalker, which achieves a 33.8% average improvement over its base model, surpassing proprietary models like Gemini-3-Flash in spatial simulation. Furthermore, SpatialWalker demonstrates robust generalization, maintaining a 4.9% average gain on out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrating the pivotal role of spatial simulation as a fundamental building block for broader spatial intelligence.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate promising capabilities in 2D visual understanding, yet remain limited in 3D spatial reasoning. Recent studies explore integrating 3D reconstruction models into MLLMs. However, these approaches either use a single geometric granularity across all tasks or adopt a fixed multi-level fusion scheme, regardless of task-specific reasoning requirements. Through systematic experiments on multi-level geometric fusion, we find that spatial tasks at different cognitive levels (e.g., distance estimation vs. route planning) require geometric representations of different granularities, revealing that fixed fusion cannot accommodate diverse spatial reasoning requirements. To bridge this gap, we propose SpatialMind, a task-adaptive framework for dynamically integrating multi-level geometric representations. SpatialMind employs GeoFus, a multi-layer geometric fusion module built around a gating network jointly conditioned on textual instructions, visual semantics, and geometric representations. This network adaptively modulates the contributions of shallow, intermediate, and deep geometric features across the layers of the LLM decoder. Furthermore, we introduce a negative-entropy regularization loss to mitigate gate collapse. Gate visualizations indicate that GeoFus adapts geometric granularity to task requirements. Experiments demonstrate that SpatialMind achieves competitive performance across spatial benchmarks, attaining a state-of-the-art score of 72.3 on VSI-Bench. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/zhaosb-star/SpatialMind.

</details>

Abstract: Our aim is to accelerate autoregressive vision-language-action models (VLAs), which generate robot actions one token at a time. Speculative decoding makes this possible: a lightweight draft model proposes several tokens at once, and the VLA verifies them in a single pass, accepting only those that match its own predictions, so decoding remains lossless. The speedup, however, is limited in robot control, where many different tokens encode nearly the same motion and a draft close to the correct action is still rejected. To address this, our approach, VSpark, is built upon three design choices: (i) rollouts collected with calibrated action noise, which expose the draft to a wider range of robot states; (ii) feature alignment, which trains the draft to match the VLA's hidden features rather than its output tokens alone; and (iii) continuation reuse, which keeps the predictions the VLA has already computed along rejected drafts as candidates for the next verification step, instead of discarding them. On four VLAs with different action tokenizers and four manipulation benchmarks, VSpark achieves 1.28x to 2.10x speedup while keeping the VLA's decoding lossless.

</details>

Abstract: E-commerce advertising video creation requires both high-level creative planning and reliable multi-modal production. However, existing methods often map product attributes directly to scripts or editing decisions without explicitly modeling advertising strategies, narrative structures, and production styles. They also have limited ability to reuse past production failures. To address these limitations, we propose $\textbf{CutAgent}$, a dual-agent framework that formulates advertising video creation as a Plan--Execute--Self-Evolve process. CutAgent comprises two collaborative agents, $\textbf{GeneAgent}$ and $\textbf{VideoAgent}$, connected by a structured creative gene that bridges product understanding and video production. GeneAgent maps multi-modal product inputs---textual attributes together with product images---to creative genes that specify narrative units and production rules. To train GeneAgent, we construct $\textbf{CutAgent-70K}$ with approximately 70K samples of product attributes/images, reverse-extracted creative genes, cleaned clip pools, and real-world CTR, and apply high-CTR supervised fine-tuning followed by CTR-guided GRPO. Guided by the generated gene, VideoAgent retrieves category-then-gene failure--correction experiences from a long-term memory as constraints for shot-script planning, then coordinates material retrieval, narration, subtitles, music, and timeline rendering. Independently, a reflection loop diagnoses quality defects, revises deficient production decisions, and writes verified corrections back to memory for later retrieval. Experiments on $\textbf{CutAgent-Eval}$ show that CutAgent outperforms representative baselines in e-commerce advertising video creation, achieving the highest online CTR of $\textbf{2.21\%}$.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion models generate high-quality images, but how well they generalize is hard to measure. Current evaluations rely on human preference and prompt-following benchmarks for text-to-image models and on distribution-level metrics such as Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) and Inception Score (IS) for class-conditional models, but these cannot tell whether models learn basic visual skills or largely match training patterns. To address this gap, we introduce the Visual Generative Lab (VGL), a controlled experimental framework for studying how diffusion models learn and generalize basic visual skills, including size, position, rotation, count, color, shape, and their compositions. By generating synthetic training data for each skill from explicit rules, VGL allows precise measurement of generalization with task-specific, rule-based metrics. Training models from scratch on these data reveals three patterns in this controlled setting: (1) with our default design, extrapolation is limited: size and position degrade within a few pixels of the training range, count reaches only about 50% accuracy on out-of-range counts, and rotation extrapolation accuracy varies widely across seeds; (2) for compositional generalization, the number of distinct basic visual skill combinations matters more than dataset size; (3) skills mostly fail independently, except position. Code and data-generation scripts: https://github.com/AmishSethi/visual-generative-lab.

</details>

Abstract: Human cognitive state (e.g., distraction, stress), gaze, and behavior interact with the driving environment in subtle ways that AI could use to assist drivers, but ground-truth labels for human cognitive state levels are costly to obtain at scale. We propose a multimodal masked-diffusion autoencoder, ID-MAE, that adds a self-supervised objective to impairment prediction: alongside predicting the impairment label as a masked token, it reconstructs masked gaze and trajectory. To probe the effects of different modalities and types of impairments on model inference, we introduce DIVERT, a driving-simulator dataset containing 103 hours of driving data (n = 109) with ten impairment inductions spanning cognitive, visual, and emotional states, recorded with gaze, vehicle state, and scene video. We demonstrate how ID-MAE affords significant improvements in impairment inference results on both the DIVERT dataset and existing datasets, and enables strong results in additional tasks, such as trajectory and gaze prediction. For location-matched pairs, our model achieves a paired AUC of 0.706 for impairment detection on DIVERT, compared to 0.590 without reconstruction loss, improving over existing baselines. We probe the design space in terms of input modalities, masking scheme, positional encoding, attention mechanism, and decoding. We also investigate the interaction between various impairments in training and inference, demonstrating that visually demanding distractions are separable from cognitive distractions. Code and data will be available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [182] [When Neural Collapse Fails to Route: Routing Drift in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22429)
*Nicolas Larue*

Abstract: Progressive neural-collapse classifiers can learn strong task-local decision boundaries while failing the global Class-IL decision. We identify the failure as routing drift: under an expanding equiangular tight frame (ETF) classifier, a held-out sample can remain separable within its true task block, yet receive its largest global score from another block. This produces high Task-IL accuracy but low Class-IL accuracy, and small replay buffers can mask the problem because stored samples are optimized repeatedly and often remain well routed. We formalize route accuracy directly from the global class argmax and use it to separate within-task discrimination from cross-task score comparability. This decomposition suggests a classifier-level correction: preserve the global ETF frame, but add class-local reliability evidence when transported class measures deviate from the neural-collapse ideal. We develop Reliability-Calibrated Routing (RCR), a lightweight post-hoc classifier that composes standardized ETF alignment with radial plausibility around transported, NC-shrunk class representatives. Across three datasets and two fixed replay-buffer budgets, this NC-consistent correction gives the best Class-IL FAA on five of six settings and reduces Class-IL forgetting on all six. The largest gains occur in harder routing regimes: up to $+6.75$ FAA over the reported reference, and up to $+8.03$ FAA and $+8.62$ final route accuracy in paired evaluations, while preserving Task-IL performance. These results position routing drift as a measurable failure mode of ETF-based continual learning and show that reliability-calibrated class evidence can repair part of the lost global routing without retraining the representation. Code and evaluation artifacts are available at https://towzeur.github.io/RCR/.

</details>


### [183] [Where Demand Meets Supply: Spatial Elastic Consensus for Low-Bandwidth Collaborative Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22439)

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Occlusions and limited sensor range make ego-only 3D perception insufficient for safe driving. Collaborative perception addresses this by enabling connected vehicles to share complementary observations, yet it remains constrained by tight vehicular communication budgets. While spatial selection is more flexible than fixed-rate feature compression, existing selectors remain largely sender-driven and waste scarce bandwidth on ego-visible targets. Furthermore, applying convolutions after masking spreads isolated tokens into their neighborhoods, silently violating low-bandwidth constraints. To address this, we propose Spatial Elastic Consensus (SEC), a bandwidth-adaptive framework centered on bilateral demand-supply consensus. Instead of unilateral token selection, the ego vehicle formulates a content-aware demand prior while each collaborator evaluates its local supply. This single-round consensus allocates the token budget to critical ego-missed evidence rather than ego-visible redundancy, without transmitting dense maps. To guarantee exact budget compliance, an anti-dilation refine-before-mask pipeline refines features prior to discrete token selection, preventing receptive-field leakage. A lightweight deformable sampler then aggregates these sparse messages with learned local offsets, compensating for the spatial context lost to token selection and mitigating inter-agent pose drift. Evaluated at a stringent 64KiB budget, SEC consistently achieves the best AP@0.7 on the simulated OPV2V and V2XSet datasets and the real-world DAIR-V2X dataset. These results demonstrate that combining bilateral consensus with budget-exact transmission is key to efficient low-bandwidth collaborative perception.

</details>


### [184] [As Student Rollouts Continue, Visual Backdoors Keep Growing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22446)
*Zane Fu,Yanzhao Zheng,Jinze Liu,Ziting Qin,Qianshan Wei,Jihuai Zhu,Yipeng Yu,Baohua Dong,Hangcheng Zhu,Ruohui Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Conventional knowledge distillation can facilitate the propagation of backdoors from teacher models to student models. However, how this risk manifests in On-Policy Distillation (OPD) remains insufficiently studied. OPD has recently emerged as an important post-training paradigm for Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Unlike traditional offline distillation, OPD does not require teacher-generated responses. Instead, it uses the teacher's probability distributions to supervise response trajectories generated online by the student. Since the initial student model typically generates benign responses and receives no explicit supervision from malicious target responses, OPD appears to offer some inherent resistance to backdoor propagation.
  In this paper, we challenge this intuition and reveal a more subtle risk of visual backdoor propagation in OPD. Through experiments across multiple model families and datasets, together with theoretical analysis, we identify three main findings. First, unlike the relatively gradual propagation observed in traditional offline distillation, backdoors rarely emerge in OPD at low poisoning ratios. However, the attack success rate rapidly approaches 100% once a critical threshold is exceeded. Second, backdoor propagation does not depend on explicit malicious responses. Instead, it is driven by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher. Finally, based on these findings, we propose a novel trigger design that enables visual backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. These findings further reveal an overlooked mechanism of backdoor propagation in OPD and highlight its potential security risks.

</details>

Abstract: Identifying whom a person looks at is central to understanding multi-party conversations, yet gaze geometry alone becomes ambiguous as more people appear. Conversations offer cues that geometry lacks, such as who is speaking and what is said, but no gaze dataset provides them for many multi-party scenes. We introduce SORBET, a benchmark of 3,340 samples from 1,578 clips with synchronized speech, transcripts, and attended person labels, covering more multi-party scenes than existing gaze datasets. Prior gaze models struggle to use these cues, which come in different forms, must be linked to the right person, and can conflict with gaze geometry. We propose Nunchi, a framework that constructs a text prompt from the transcript and the outputs of gaze and speaker models, links every cue to the candidates with shared labels, and lets a vision-language model (VLM) weigh gaze geometry against conversational cues to identify the attended person. Nunchi outperforms the strongest existing method by 4.8 percentage points. On SORBET, conversational cues help whereas additional visual cues do not, and the gains hold across three VLMs, with automatic transcripts, and on two existing datasets.

</details>


### [186] [Sample-Aligned Decoders for Latent Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22458)
*Yu Lei,Yi Li,Hongcheng Gao,Yipeng Wei,Liang Li,Li Su,Qingming Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Latent generative models synthesize images by decoding generated latents, yet their decoder is trained only to reconstruct encoder latents and never sees the latents it decodes at test time, so the residual errors of the generator are rendered directly into the final image. Existing remedies retrain the generator, add cost at every sampling step, or make the decoder robust to perturbed real latents, assuming that generator errors resemble such perturbations. We find that they do not. Generated latents lie on the encoder latent manifold as closely as real ones, yet follow a different distribution. The decoder should therefore be adapted to the distribution of generated latents, which raises two challenges: (1) unpaired supervision, since a generated latent has no ground-truth image, and (2) latent-space drift, since a decoder freed from paired targets forgets the latent space it must decode. We propose Sample-Aligned Decoders (SAD), a post-training framework that fine-tunes only the decoder of a frozen generator at no inference cost. Generated-Sample Distribution Alignment matches decoded generated latents to unpaired real images in a frozen foundation-model feature space, and Encoder Anchor Preservation keeps the decoder close to the original one on encoder latents. Our analysis shows that the alignment is effective only on generated latents, where it corrects each sample in proportion to how much more often the generator produces such latents than the encoder; on encoder latents it can only work against the anchor. SAD improves four state-of-the-art ImageNet generators across three tokenizers, e.g., FID-50K from 1.15 to 0.98 for REPA-E, complements discriminator guidance, and extends to text-to-image generation. Our code and models are available at https://torpedo2003.github.io/SAD/.

</details>


### [187] [TextSphere: Efficient Text-to-Image Generation via Shared Hyperspherical Alignment and Uniformity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23155)

Abstract: Recent advances in text-to-image generation have substantially improved image quality, particularly through diffusion-based methods that require multiple inference steps. In contrast, Sphere Encoder offers an efficient generative framework capable of producing images with fewer forward steps. However, extending it to free-form text is nontrivial, because its class-conditioned design lacks cross-modal alignment. To address this challenge, we propose TextSphere, an efficient text-to-image generative framework that extends Sphere Encoder with language conditioning and cross-modal alignment. We incorporate pretrained CLIP text embeddings through adaptive layer normalization (AdaLN) to guide image generation while retaining the original spherical sampling mechanism. To bridge the cross-modal representation gap, we introduce a Spherical Semantic Alignment and Uniformity (SSAU) objective that jointly promotes semantic alignment and representation uniformity on the hypersphere. Experiments on CUB-200-2011, MS-COCO-2014, and Flickr30k demonstrate that TextSphere consistently reduces generation FID (gFID) over the text-conditioned baseline while achieving reconstruction FID (rFID) below 1.0. With only 4 and 6 sampling steps, TextSphere achieves lower gFID than the compared diffusion- and GAN-based models trained from scratch on CUB-200-2011 and MS-COCO-2014, respectively. We further observe that improved reconstruction fidelity does not necessarily yield better generation quality. In addition, we use Partial Least Squares Singular Value Decomposition (PLS-SVD) to analyze cross-modal alignment and modality gap, offering insight into the effectiveness of our approach.

</details>

Abstract: Animated 3D blockouts support previsualization and provide structural conditions for video generation, but manually reconstructing them from reference videos is costly. This limits both their use by creators and the production of paired training data. We introduce MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes for evaluating camera motion, spatial layout, and character blocking. We propose MAVE, a video-to-blockout framework combining Metric observation, Agentic modeling, and evidence-based VErification. It first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system. An agent then constructs an editable environment under constraints from these observations, while programmatic checks report inconsistencies to guide refinement. The outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points across three agents over their corresponding vanilla baselines. With DeepSeek-1017, it achieves a score of 73.2 at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot, outperforming the strongest vanilla baseline while reducing total reconstruction time by 53%. We also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs for model training, and will release it publicly. Creators can change viewpoints and character paths, then render the edited scenes to condition video generation. Code is available at https://github.com/yfChang-cv/MAVE.

</details>


### [189] [Disagree to Prune: Reallocating Visual Tokens Where a Small VLM Dissents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22472)
*Yanchao Li,Jiaqing Xie,Ben Gao,Yusu Zhang,Beilun Wang,Yanbo Wang,Xiaohua Xu,Zhongyu Huang,Tianfan Fu,Nanqing Dong*

Abstract: Visual token pruning speeds up large vision-language models (VLMs) by keeping only a part of their visual tokens. Most existing methods improve the ranking of the tokens and then answer every question once with the same budget. With strong rankings, pruning hurts only a few questions, and signals computed on the kept tokens see little of what it removed. We find that, on most of these questions, a small VLM given the full image disagrees with the pruned answer, since it still sees the missing evidence. We propose PRUDENT, which first answers with about half the budget and spends the saved tokens on re-answering the disagreeing questions at twice the budget. We show when such re-answering pays and why the small VLM should read the full image. On 18 benchmarks, PRUDENT outperforms eight existing pruning methods at the same average budget of visual tokens. Its lead widens as the budget tightens, and holds across five InternVL sizes. PRUDENT also has the highest average in two more model families. The disagreement of the small VLM flags most pruning failures of all eight methods, and re-answering on it also improves the two strongest with their own rankings. Other checkers give similar gains, and the extra tokens go where the first pass hurts the answer. These results suggest that identifying which pruned answers are trustworthy provides an additional lever for visual token pruning beyond the ranking of tokens. Code and data are available at https://github.com/LiZaiyuan0619/PRUDENT.

</details>


### [190] [LeViT-UNet++: Towards Balanced Fine-Grained Details and Context for Efficient Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22482)

Abstract: Efficient medical image segmentation is essential for clinical applications requiring fast and accurate delineation, such as surgical navigation, radiation therapy, and real-time image-guided intervention. Despite substantial advances in convolutional neural networks and Transformers, attaining high accuracy under limited computational resources remains difficult, because reducing model complexity often sacrifices fine-grained structural details and global context modeling, yielding an unfavorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. To address this, we propose LeViT-UNet++, an efficient encoder-decoder architecture that better balances local detail preservation and global context modeling. Specifically, we introduce a Haar Wavelet Multi-Scale Downsampling (HWMD) module that decomposes feature representations into low- and high-frequency components to extract multi-scale structural information, thereby mitigating detail loss during downsampling. We further develop a depthwise convolution-based decoder that integrates multi-scale global representations from Transformer blocks with local spatial features, enabling progressive feature refinement while reducing computational overhead. Evaluated on four benchmarks (Synapse, ACDC, LA2018, and MSD Heart) under both 2D and 3D settings, LeViT-UNet++ achieves competitive accuracy with a compact parameter footprint and fast inference. On Synapse, for instance, it attains a DSC of 76.57% with only 8.025M parameters and 241.72 FPS, confirming a favorable accuracy-efficiency balance. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining wavelet-based multi-scale feature extraction with lightweight local-global feature fusion for efficient medical segmentation. The source code is available at https://github.com/apple1986/levit_unet_pp.

</details>

Abstract: Personalized text-to-image diffusion models can reproduce a person from a handful of photographs. Image protection perturbs each image before publication so that fine-tuning on it fails, but it optimizes every perturbation from scratch, so its cost grows with the number of images. We present the protective diffusion model (PDM), which is trained once and samples the perturbation of a new image over many denoising steps. PDM distills an iterative teacher and is then post-trained with the target residual projection (TRP) loss as a reward, a stronger objective than existing ones. PDM outperforms nine baselines on faces and artworks and nearly matches its teacher at a tenth of the cost.

</details>

Abstract: Reward models for instruction-guided video editing can favor temporal consistency at the expense of motion. We study this failure, termed \emph{motion collapse}, using controlled motion-suppression variants of real edits. On VE-Bench, the evaluated composite reward prefers a motion-suppressed candidate in $97\%$ of cases. Learned video rewards and a VLM judge also struggle with mild suppression, while latent optimization and reward-guided fine-tuning exploit this weakness to increase reward as motion declines. A linear probe of our base reward backbone performs near chance on mild suppression, motivating an explicit inter-frame dynamics input. We propose a motion-aware reward model that combines an RGB frame-difference stream with preference training against motion-suppressed and fabricated-motion negatives. A distillation anchor preserves alignment with human quality judgments. On the VE-Bench motion ladder, our model reduces the rate of selecting motion-suppressed candidates to $0\%$ while achieving a Spearman correlation of $0.57$ with human quality ratings. It also detects unseen suppression variants and preserves motion during reward-guided fine-tuning on two editing pipelines. These results support explicitly modeling motion in rewards for video editing.

</details>

Abstract: Medical vision-language models (VLMs) capture cross-modal relationships between image and text, yet their adaptation to institution-specific clinical tasks remains underexplored. In this study, we fine-tuned a pretrained 3D medical VLM for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) classification using paired abdominal CT scans and radiology reports from a different institution with acquisition characteristics that differ from the model's original pretraining corpus. We compared two adaptation strategies: full fine-tuning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), motivated by the common use of PEFT to reduce computational cost and enable adaptation under limited-data constraints. Both approaches achieve strong downstream HCC classification performance despite the cross-institutional domain shift, with PEFT reaching an AUC of 0.79 and F1 of 0.70, and full fine-tuning achieving an AUC of 0.82 and F1 of 0.71. Although these results remain below the performance reported by supervised HCC classification approaches, our method achieves improvements over the pretrained model through CT-report contrastive learning without requiring lesion-level annotations or HCC classification labels as training targets. Full fine-tuning converges rapidly but overfits within a few epochs, whereas PEFT (ConvLoRA for the image encoder and LoRA for the text encoder) attains comparable performance while updating approximately 1% of the model parameters, although requiring more training steps. We also examine the role of contrastive temperature, observing that temperature initialization significantly affects classification performance. This study demonstrates that a 3D medical VLM can be efficiently adapted to institution-specific HCC classification using self-supervised CT-report contrastive learning, while highlighting the practical trade-offs between full fine-tuning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning.

</details>

Abstract: Visual place recognition (VPR) faces critical challenges in handling extreme environmental variations while meeting the computational constraints of practical applications. Current methods predominantly address these challenges by either scaling up model capacity or employing computationally intensive reranking stages, creating a significant efficiency bottleneck. To overcome this limitation, we propose EfficientVPR, a lightweight one-stage framework that achieves speed-accuracy trade-offs through two key innovations: i) a scene-aware visual prompt tuning method which adapts pretrained features with less parameters while dynamically adjusting to sample-specific characteristics, and ii) an instance-dependent key local feature enhancement module that further reinforces discriminative regions. Comprehensive evaluations on Pitts250k, MSLS, Eynsham, AmsterTime and SVOX demonstrate that our method establishes a new SOTA for DINOv2-small models by outperforming all same-scale competitors, and delivers a $73\times$ speedup with 60\% lower-dimensional features while maintaining competitive (within 2.5% average R@1 gap) against the SOTA DINOv2-large-based two-stage method. Our code will be available at https://github.com/WiniTang/EfficientVPR.

Abstract: Hyperspectral image (HSI) and LiDAR data provide complementary spectral and elevation information for land-cover classification. However, the spatially varying class-discriminative reliability of modality features is rarely modeled explicitly, allowing weak or ambiguous features to adversely affect cross-modal interaction and fusion. To address this issue, we propose ReDF, a reliability-driven framework that quantifies the reliability of each modality's features at each spatial location through the strength of class-related evidence they provide, and uses it to guide feature correction and fusion. Specifically, a Reliability-Guided Feature Correction module introduces a class prototype bank for each modality and converts feature-prototype similarities into class evidence for reliability estimation. The estimated reliabilities are used to construct a reliability-weighted shared representation and to guide bidirectional cross-modal feature correction based on neighborhood agreement, refining modality features with weak class evidence through information from the other modality. Building on these outputs, a Reliability-Guided Feature Reweighting module conditions a low-rank modulation on the estimated reliabilities and the cross-modal disagreement, and applies it to the corrected modality-specific descriptors to adaptively rescale each modality's contribution to the fused representation used for final classification. Experiments on three public HSI-LiDAR datasets show higher mean overall accuracy and Kappa than the compared methods.

</details>


### [196] [ATS: Anti-Aliased Triangle Splatting with Exact Per-Primitive Integrals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22512)
*Brent Zoomers,Joni Vanherck,Leander Winters,Jeroen Put,Lode Jorissen,Nick Michiels*

Main category: cs.CV

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Triangle Splatting represents scenes using semi-transparent triangles, but it evaluates each triangle at the pixel center and culls any triangle with an inradius smaller than one pixel. The former causes severe aliasing, while the latter culls most of the scene at lower resolutions. We show that the integral of the Triangle Splatting window over a pixel admits an exact closed-form solution. The angle bisectors split the triangle into three sub-triangles, in each of which the window is a power of the distance to one edge. Each piece therefore has a closed-form antiderivative, and Green's theorem reduces the integral and its gradients to one term per side of the sub-triangle clipped against the pixel. The integral over the whole triangle, which we show has a closed form, then allows conservative culling, removing only triangles that cannot meet the renderer's alpha cutoff at any pixel. This exact formulation anti-aliases existing models without retraining, exhibits less temporal aliasing than 2x2 supersampling, and enables multi-scale training, matching or outperforming supersampled training at reduced resolutions.

</details>


### [197] [Fast Training-Free Low-Light Video Enhancement with a Frozen Image Diffusion Prior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22551)

Abstract: Pretrained image diffusion models can enhance low-light video without low-light training data and without retraining for a new camera. ZVRD, the only published training-free method for low-light video, is too slow to use: our implementation needs over a minute per $256 \times 256$ frame. We present a causal, training-free pipeline that adds two temporal components to a frozen MR. Illuminate image prior. Temporal input fusion recursively averages motion-compensated low-light frames before diffusion inversion. Late-start offset propagation runs the full sampling trajectory only on key frames. Each non-key frame inverts the low-noise part of its own trajectory and adds an enhancement offset warped from the latest key frame. At ZVRD's own resolution the pipeline is $261\times$ faster than ZVRD and scores better on all five metrics we report. On 13 held-out SDSD clips at $1280 \times 720$, it improves on framewise eight-step processing by 0.55 dB in PSNR and 0.034 in LPIPS and halves the warping error, in 10% less time. Measurements inside the model show why, and a linear-Gaussian model of inversion explains them. Fusion removes frame-to-frame noise before inversion amplifies it. A flow error perturbs the transported offset ten times less than it perturbs a transported state. The perceptual gain does not transfer to low resolution: at $346 \times 260$, LPIPS is worse by 0.028. At 0.65 frames per second, the pipeline is an offline method.

</details>


### [198] [Strong Fused Performance Can Mask Branch Collapse in a Dual-View Heart-Sound Classifier: A Three-Part Diagnostic Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22556)

Abstract: Fusion models are usually judged by their fused score, a practice that assumes that every branch has learned something useful. We show that this assumption can fail silently in dual-view heart-sound classification. In AudioFuse, which fuses a spectrogram transformer with a waveform CNN, the spectrogram branch is unused despite strong fused performance when both branches are trained from scratch. We introduce a three-part diagnostic for branch collapse: whether deleting a branch changes the fused score, whether a linear probe on its features beats an untrained copy of the same branch, and whether the branch can learn its view when trained alone. Deleting the branch has negligible effect, and probing its features shows little improvement over an identically initialised, untrained copy. Yet the same architecture trained alone exceeds 0.9 AUC, so its view is learnable, while the jointly trained model is near chance when it must rely on this branch. Update statistics and an initialisation experiment point to an early optimisation imbalance. Moving a strong artificial label cue from one input to the other can switch which branch the model relies on, even though the branch architectures stay the same. We observe branch collapse on PhysioNet, but the full diagnostic is not met on ICBHI or CirCor. Fused performance alone therefore cannot establish that every branch learned a meaningful representation, and we recommend reporting branch deletion and an untrained-control probe alongside it.

</details>


### [199] [Plausible Videos Can Lead to Poor Decisions: Diagnosing and CURE-ing World Models for Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22561)
*Raphael C. Kim,Michele Santacatterina,Jingsen Zhu,Ramin Zabih*

Abstract: Action-conditioned world models are increasingly used as world models for planning, yet a world model that makes plausible, realistic-looking videos that are consistent with the training data can produce poor decisions. We address two key questions: (1) when can we trust a world model for planning, and (2) what should we do when we cannot? We introduce CURE (Certify, Upweight, Rollout, Explore), a certificate-guided technique that assesses whether a world model can be trusted for planning and determines what to do next: whether to upweight the training data to debias the model; roll out more samples at test time; or explore by collecting new data where support (how well the training data cover the planner's decisions) is missing. We deploy CURE across multiple domains, spanning simulated physics based on PhyWorld, MuJoCo arm control, and Language-Table pushing in simulation and on real videos. The decisions our certificate trusts have lower regret than those it does not (e.g., 0.06 vs. 0.38 on Language-Table sim and 0.07 vs. 0.38 on MuJoCo contact), whereas video perceptual quality does not identify good decisions. By focusing on decision quality rather than perceptual quality, CURE lowers regret over the best pessimistic decision rule by 10-25% across support levels, and over world-action models, search and policy learning by 41-64%, given the same data.

</details>


### [200] [Lost in Read-out: Locating the Image Quality Gap in Vision-Language Models with a Closed-Form Edit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22564)
*Zhuangzi Li,Shilv Cai,Jian Jin,Zhiyi Pan,Haihong Xiao,Weisi Lin*

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are widely used as zero-shot image quality assessors, yet their good-vs-poor answers agree only moderately with human opinion. We ask whether these models fail to see quality or fail to say it. Layer-wise probes on five VLMs point to the second: quality is linearly decodable from the vision encoder and still from the last language layer, far better than the answer reveals, and about a hundred labels recover most of it. The loss lies mainly in the final read-out, which partly reads something else: relative to human opinion, it over-rewards colorfulness and under-rewards sharpness on all five models. We test this by intervention: if the read-out is at fault, a closed-form edit of a single last-layer neuron, fitted to many prompts at once, should close the gap and no more. Both hold. Under prompts never used for fitting, the edit recovers 86--100\% of what a ridge head on the last-layer state, fitted under the same prompts, finds beyond the answer on KonIQ. Where that gap is small, it gains little, too little for our transfer criterion on the two largest models; where the head itself is below the answer, it lowers the score; and it works only when written from the middle of the language model on. Unlike unconstrained fine-tuning, the edit leaves perception question answering intact. It does not carry over to yes-or-no or rating questions, and it reverses the good--poor contrast after sentiment-laden sentences. Code will be made available.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) provide a unified interface for language-conditioned visual perception. In pixel-to-sequence grounding, autoregressive coordinate generation requires multiple sequential decoding steps per box, increasing inference cost with the number of instances. Pixel-to-embedding methods reduce this overhead but lack explicit spatial encoding when using a shared special token. We introduce SemGeo, a token-efficient visual grounding framework that represents each instance with a single grid token for coarse localization. A geometric decoder reuses visual features and hidden states to recover precise discrete box coordinates in parallel. To train proposal generation and box localization together, we introduce Gaussian soft labels that encode coordinate distances during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Reinforcement post-training further combines box-set rewards with coordinate supervision on model-generated proposals. This stage trains the language model and geometric decoder on their own predictions to mitigate the mismatch between teacher-forced training and inference. Across eight benchmarks spanning object detection, referring object detection, and document layout grounding, SemGeo achieves the highest F1@mIou among compared MLLMs on six benchmarks. It also delivers $2.07\times$ and $1.25\times$ the mean box-generation throughput of representative grounding baselines under vLLM and Transformers, respectively, while achieving higher accuracy in both comparisons.

</details>

Abstract: A pixel-sparse adversary may change a few pixels of an image to any value. Robustness to such attacks is currently measured with an ensemble, sparse-AutoAttack (sAA), because its members fail in different places: sparse projected gradient descent (sPGD) trusts a first-order model that a full pixel change breaks, and sparse random search (Sparse-RS) ignores the gradient even where it is informative. We show that both attacks are local searches, each a proposal distribution plus an acceptance rule, and ask what such a search can learn from the gradient. A Taylor bound says what to measure, and measurements at the states the search visits give five rules. The sign of the gradient is its most reliable use at a single pixel and sets a new pixel's value. Its ranking of candidate pixels should be followed loosely, and its ranking of the pixels to remove, which is often more reliable, can be followed more closely. A pixel should not be proposed twice before a move is accepted, and acceptance should be decided by the loss alone. These rules define Gradient-guided Random Search (GRS), which runs once and costs one forward and one backward pass per step. On 13 CIFAR-10 configurations and 5 ImageNet models, GRS matches or beats the best single member of sAA and is within one point of sAA itself or stronger on 17 of 18 settings, and it is significantly stronger than sAA on 8. It uses 3 instead of up to 7 forward-pass units, and on CIFAR-10 it runs 3.7 to 5.0 times faster than the members of sAA run in full.

</details>


### [203] [Sub-Meter Canopy Height Synthesis from Sentinel-2 via Anchored Residual Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22636)

Abstract: Canopy height models (CHMs) - per-pixel maps of vegetation top height - are essential for forest carbon accounting, wildfire fuel mapping, and tree-level ecology, yet sub-meter CHMs require airborne LiDAR refreshed on multi-year national cycles. We produce 0.5 m CHMs from free 10 m Sentinel-2 imagery, a 20$\times$ cross-modal super-resolution task where optical reflectance must predict geometric height it does not directly observe. Motivated by the perception-distortion trade-off, we derive two operating points with shared conditioning. A deterministic regressor (Anchor) targets low distortion; a conditional flow matching model, trained on the Anchor's residual in a learned CHM latent space (AR-flow), targets perceptual fidelity. Trained on 197k LiDAR-paired tiles spanning 69 ecoregions across the contiguous United States and evaluated on 2,530 held-out tiles against eight baselines, the Anchor achieves 2.83 m MAE using only free satellite input. The AR-flow reduces sliced Wasserstein distance from the Anchor's 1.13 to 0.208, at a higher MAE of 3.68 m. Crown-scale statistics improve, but strict individual-tree detection remains weak: generated sub-pixel detail represents plausible structure rather than measured individual trees. Quarterly continental refresh becomes tractable, letting satellite monitoring inform timing and extent for more expensive data acquisitions.

</details>

Abstract: Modern video moment retrieval systems often propose windows that overlap the target event, yet still select the wrong one. We study this scorer bottleneck on QVHighlights with a fixed QD-DETR-derived temporal expert: the parent-aware temporal expert reaches 64.71 R1@0.5, while diagnostic oracles over the same candidate family reach 75.68 R1@0.5 within the parent-reranked pool and 94.97 R1@0.5 over the full 100-candidate evidence pool. We introduce OVET-Consensus, a rank-preserving counterfactual event verification layer that keeps the temporal expert's ranked list as the default and permits only high-confidence rank-1 overrides from a policy calibrated on held-out training videos. The verifier uses parent-aware interval evidence, structured Qwen3-VL event judgments, VideoLLaMA3 pairwise temporal signals, and dense temporal evidence, but never lets those signals freely reorder the top-K list. On QVHighlights validation, OVET-Consensus improves the parent-aware temporal expert from 64.71 to 65.35 R1@0.5 with a positive paired-bootstrap interval, while mAP is largely unchanged, from 42.67 to 42.74; it makes 20 overrides, with 15 helpful corrections and 3 harmful corrections. The results point to a practical constraint on verifier design: semantic compatibility is useful evidence, but temporal grounding still requires deciding where the event starts, changes, and ends inside a visually compatible scene.

</details>


### [205] [PlaceTally: A Test-Time Memory Model for Constructing Place Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22608)

Abstract: Counting distinct objects in a video is hard when the camera returns to places it has seen. A vision-language model that reads the whole video answers with a few small numbers, and one that reads it in parts counts an object again in every part that shows it. Object trackers need the category before the video is read, or a fixed vocabulary. We observe that a memory for counting does not need to know what it is looking for, only where it has been: objects rarely leave a room between visits, so parts of a video grouped by place share almost no objects, and a place can be recognised before any question is asked. Building on this, we introduce PlaceTally, a question-blind test-time memory model that constructs a place graph for each video. Each video fragment joins the place of its best earlier match or opens a new place, because a strong match is rarely wrong while a true return often matches weakly. A frozen vision-language model counts each place once from at most 128 of its frames, so every read has a bounded input and the memory grows with the number of places; the graph is built 15 times faster than real time. On RevisitCount, our benchmark of real room re-recordings, it scores 62.5% mean relative accuracy on test rooms and 61.2% on held-out rooms, against 51.2% and 44.3% for the same model reading the whole video. It stays level as rooms return more or less often, while a fixed correction of summed visits moves from 38.5% to 61.4%.

</details>


### [206] [REVEAL: Reconstructing Video Amodal Content via Language and Flow Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22645)
