Abstract: Analysis of fibrous structures in SEM images is a routine task in biomedical engineering and materials science. However, most available methods for fiber quantification still rely on classical image binarization followed by simple post-processing. These approaches often produce unreliable results in images with weak boundaries, heterogeneous contrast, and densely packed fibers. Here, we introduce Fiberpose, a framework that reformulates fiber analysis as direct regression of a smooth distance-transform (DT) field, together with a novel set of geometry-based algorithms specifically adapted to this representation. The predicted field directly encodes fiber geometry, while the accompanying algorithms enable instance reconstruction and measurement of diameter and orientation within a unified workflow. The framework is implemented as a standalone Python desktop application and a web tool https://fiberpose.org. On an independent dataset of fibrous SEM images, Fiberpose achieved state-of-the-art instance-segmentation accuracy in comparison with SAM2 and Cellpose-SAM, and more accurate diameter measurements than DiameterJ, Fibar and GIFT. The method remains effective in dense and weakly contrasted regions and shows qualitative generalization across microscopy modalities not represented in the training data. Fiberpose therefore provides an interpretable geometric representation and an automated pipeline for reproducible quantitative characterization of fibrous materials.

</details>

Abstract: Ensembles of diverse Large Vision Language Models have demonstrated performance beyond that of single models across multiple datasets and tasks. However, the necessary aggregation process usually relies on training, parameter fitting, or complex task-specific pipelines. Furthermore, evaluation is limited, making a fair comparison difficult. We create a unified pipeline where we perform candidate response selection: we build an ensemble by selecting the best answer from a set provided by different models. We comprehensively evaluate across 34 different means of confidence estimation, 24 ensemble configurations, 20 datasets, and two prompt specifications. We show that simple training and parameter-free approaches can be sufficient, even for ensembles of different architectures. These simple self-assessment approaches rely solely on each constituent model's confidence, without explicit calibration, yet are able to perform better than said constituent models. They are also competitive with more involved alternatives on standard metrics such as accuracy, including on open-ended problems. However, we also discover that dataset, ensemble composition, and prompt wording are key confounding variables insufficiently explored by prior work that significantly affect ensemble performance, such that the ensemble can perform worse than its best constituent model. These must be optimized for to get the most out of ensembles, at the cost of limiting their generalizability. We recommend that practitioners consider training-free ensembles only if deployment conditions can be precisely characterized, with Best-of-N configurations of strong models being a good starting point.

</details>

Abstract: Building change detection (BCD) relies on dense annotations that are costly to collect across regions. A few annotated examples offer a more economical way to specify the target, but change maps provide incomplete building supervision: they label only changed buildings and leave the building-containing date unspecified. Our key observation is that these annotations nevertheless reveal features that recognize buildings in other images, including unchanged ones. We present Linear INduced Discriminants (LIND), a training-free framework that turns regional feature contrasts into a reusable linear building classifier. Using a single frozen vision transformer, it unifies building recognition and temporal comparison in the same feature space. This approach requires neither text prompts nor an auxiliary segmentation model. Only one parameter is tuned on a single validation dataset and then kept fixed across datasets and shot settings. Extensive experiments demonstrate strong detection accuracy, cross-dataset transfer, and efficient inference, with extensions to other semantic classes and broader land-cover changes. Change annotations thus provide a compact visual specification for detection in new scenes. Code: https://github.com/lzcn/lind

</details>

Abstract: Zero-shot whole-slide image (WSI) prediction aims to generalize to new pathology tasks without parameter updates at deployment. MI-Zero makes these predictions by pooling patch-text similarities. Conditional weight generation, combined with supervision from diverse pathology tasks, offers an alternative: learning to construct predictive rules from task descriptions. We introduce MI-Hyper, a hypernetwork pretrained across mixed tasks to generate the aggregation and classification weights of multiple instance learning (MIL) models. We first train the generator and a shared MIL basis jointly using source labels. We then freeze the basis and refine the generator. The generated task weights are reused across cases. Across 308 tasks from 52 pathology datasets, MI-Hyper improves mean AUC, balanced accuracy and macro-F1 over MI-Zero by 1.85, 1.17 and 0.57 percentage points, respectively. On some molecular prediction tasks, both AUC and balanced accuracy improve by more than 20 points. These results support task-conditioned weight generation as a promising approach to weight-space transfer for long-sequence modeling.

</details>

Abstract: Spectral band synthesis is an important research topic in remote sensing. The Brazilian satellites CBERS-4, CBERS-4A, and AMAZONIA-1 provide high temporal resolution (1-3 days) visible and near-infrared (VNIR) imagery through the Wide-Field Imager (WFI), but lack short-wave infrared (SWIR) bands. Recent deep learning methods have shown promising results for spectral synthesis, but challenges remain for cross-satellite generalization and spectrally distant bands. This paper presents a conditional Generative Adversarial Network (cGAN) with an attention-enhanced U-net generator and a multi-loss objective to synthesize Sentinel-2/MSI SWIR bands 11 (SWIR-1) and 12 (SWIR-2) from WFI VNIR inputs. Experiments used 4,125 patches for training and cross-validation and 1,026 unseen patches for testing, with hyperparameters optimized using a subset of the training data. On SWIR-1, the model achieved SSIM of 0.900, PSNR of 31.44 dB, and MAE of 0.019 in TOA reflectance. On SWIR-2, it achieved SSIM of 0.917, PSNR of 33.35 dB, and MAE of 0.015. Benchmarking was performed on the test dataset, with each model optimized separately prior to evaluation. SWIR-cGAN outperformed the baselines, particularly for SWIR-2. The ablation study identified the multi-loss as the main performance contributor, while the attention and residual modules provided additional improvements. Our results demonstrate the feasibility of synthesizing SWIR bands from WFI imagery. The SWIR-cGAN is available at https://github.com/marujore/swir-cgan.

</details>

Abstract: Merging multiple adapters into a single image generation model reduces deployment costs while preserving diverse task capabilities or personalization modes. State-of-the-art methods such as RegMean, Mix-of-Show, and Subspace Routing fit the outputs of the merged model to those of the individual adapters using ordinary least squares (OLS). We show that this reconstruction objective systematically shrinks task-specific signals whenever the activation distributions of different tasks overlap, producing a merged adapter that is too weak for downstream use. We derive a decomposition that quantifies this shrinkage and propose rescaling the merged update to restore signal strength. On multi-subject personalization with up to 30 merged adapters, rescaling substantially improves subject fidelity over unscaled baselines. Our approach also improves results on style transfer, concept erasure, image classification, and LLM-based text classification.

</details>

Abstract: Goal-conditioned planning with image observations and goals requires predictions that distinguish visual states and action consequences. Spiking neural networks offer sparse computation, but single-step spikes can obscure motion cues and merge actions with different physical outcomes. We propose the Spiking Latent World Model (SpikWM), an end-to-end joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) with one simulation step per image and one neural update per latent position. Spiking Regional Encoding retains spatial cues through regional pooling. Bi-Spike Action Encoding combines positive and negative binary responses, while Causal Membrane Prediction carries subthreshold evidence across the latent history with learned group-wise time constants. The encoder and predictor are trained jointly on reward-free image--action trajectories using next-embedding prediction and SIGReg. With a common planner, SpikWM achieves mean success rates of $88.67\%$, $97.33\%$, $99.33\%$, and $86.67\%$ on Push-T, Two-Room, Reacher, and OGBench-Cube, respectively, outperforming two spiking baselines. Push-T ablations support each module, and controlled replays examine responses to appearance and physical state changes. Under a matched planning budget, SpikWM reduces estimated arithmetic energy by $75.6\%$ relative to LeWM. Code will be released at https://github.com/ShawnYE618/SpikWM.

</details>

Abstract: Biomedical image segmentation must keep up with new anatomical structures, subjects, imaging modalities, and acquisition protocols, yet dense expert annotations are scarce and models trained on one cohort often struggle to transfer. In-context segmentation (ICS) offers a way out: a new structure is specified by a few annotated exemplars at inference time. However, reliable correspondence remains challenging in biomedical images, where adjacent structures often share similar appearances, and variations across subjects and imaging conditions further obscure their distinctions. We introduce CAST, a training-free framework built on a frozen DINOv3 backbone to calibrate fine-grained correspondences. Its stability test improves feature reliability at both global and patch-specific levels, while its coherence test assesses whether candidate correspondences are supported by consistent local structural context. Together, these tests enable more reliable correspondence across diverse anatomical structures and imaging conditions. Across eight biomedical benchmarks spanning 2D and 3D segmentation, cross-subject, and cross-modality transfer, CAST outperforms the strongest overall baseline by 12.34 percentage points in Dice and ranks first across all six volumetric settings. The versatility of CAST further opens up opportunities for challenging biomedical applications, including 2D-to-3D segmentation and atlas label transfer.

</details>

Abstract: Most AI-generated video detectors analyze decoded pixels and overlook how videos are produced. We show that causal video autoencoders leave an architecture-specific rhythm in compressed bitstreams: their temporal stride concentrates unpredictable content at latent-chunk boundaries, so rate-distortion optimized encoders allocate bits periodically. Without decoding pixels, we observe four-frame pulses in CogVideoX and Wan2.2 and eight-frame pulses in LTX-Video, which are absent from content-matched camera footage. Passing camera footage through a generator's autoencoder alone induces the pulse, supporting its proposed origin. A training-free peak-ratio statistic separates generated from camera video (AUC 0.84-0.94) and persists across tested quantization settings and H.264/H.265 encoding. Frame-size traces also identify four commercial generators, with 89.2%, 84.1%, and 83.0% accuracy under H.264, H.265, and AV1, beating the pixel-based UnivFD baseline (72.7-73.6%). Our results directly link generative architecture to measurable coding demand.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (MOPD) enables a single student to absorb the skills of several domain teachers by learning from its own generated responses. We present Multimodal Model Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation Reasoner (M3OPD-Reasoner), a fully open recipe for post-training vision-language models (VLMs) over three reasoning domains: image STEM, image perception, and video. We curate two datasets, M3OPD-SFT-1.35M for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and M3OPD-RL-193K for reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable rewards. On these data, we compare post-training recipes that start from Qwen3.5-9B: training one model on the mixed data of all domains (MixSFT and MixRL), and training one teacher per domain with Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), directly or after an SFT warm-up, and distilling the teachers into one student with MOPD. We evaluate on 21 image STEM, image perception, and video reasoning benchmarks after we decontaminate the training data against them. The comparison establishes MOPD of teachers trained with GSPO directly from Qwen3.5-9B as the best recipe: the student closely matches each teacher on its own domain and is on average above the mixed-training baselines on the same prompts. Since MOPD already merges the domain capabilities that MixRL would otherwise have to learn, its student is a stronger starting point for RL, and we ask whether the same data can carry the model further from there: we add MixRL on top of the MOPD student, on the same prompts and with the same settings, which, to our knowledge, no prior VLM recipe has studied. The same prompts still improve the MOPD student, with no collapse, and give our main model, M3OPD-Reasoner-9B. MOPD and MixRL can therefore work as consecutive stages that get more out of the same data. We release all data, the training code, and the checkpoints of every stage.

</details>

Abstract: This study developed and evaluated a computer vision model for automated ethological behavior classification of broiler chickens in climate-controlled poultry houses. Using publicly available videos preprocessed and manually annotated into three classes (sitting, standing, and feeding), the YOLOv11 model was trained and deployed on an Android application via TFLite conversion. Exploratory analysis ensured dataset consistency and visual quality, while evaluation showed class-dependent performance: higher robustness for static postures (sitting, AP=0.531; precision=100%) and lower accuracy for dynamic behaviors (feeding, AP=0.292; recall=0.20). Tests with unseen images demonstrated significant improvements, achieving accuracies of 98.04% for sitting, 83.33% for standing, and 67.65% for feeding. The model showed effective generalization in real farm environments, supporting its potential for continuous behavioral monitoring, early welfare assessments, and decision-making in precision poultry farming. These findings establish a technical foundation for future mobile-based applications at commercial scale, reinforcing the role of computer vision in automated animal welfare evaluation.

</details>


### [266] [When a Larger Guidance Scale Repairs Text-to-Video Failures and When It Does Not](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23025)

Abstract: When a text-to-video diffusion model ignores part of a prompt, the usual first fix is to raise the classifier-free guidance scale. This paper is a measurement study of when that fix works. On Mochi, the same increase repairs failures on rare compositions ("a snowy beach") beyond what generating again does, does not repair negations ("a highway with no vehicles"), where it fixes fewer failures than generating again does, and changes nothing for events that must happen late in a clip ("a sandcastle that a wave washes flat"). Which of these happens can be known in advance, from the prompt and one inexpensive score. The score, which we call demand, measures how strongly the denoiser reacts to the part of the prompt that the failed video leaves out, and it is read from the first quarter of denoising, before any video exists. Our main finding is a condition: raising the scale is effective only when the prompt contains no negation and no event tied to a specific time, and when demand is sufficiently high, with recovery clearly higher above a fitted threshold than below it. Published corrections for the three failure types, used as fixed probes, behave as this account predicts. A short decision rule follows: before generating again, a user can tell whether the most common fix is worth trying, and what to use when it is not.

</details>

Abstract: Underwater object detection is often limited by scarce and imbalanced training data and by strong appearance variations caused by turbidity, lighting, and differences between sensors. Existing data augmentation strategies, such as geometric or color augmentation or fully synthetic image generation with Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs), either lack semantic realism, fail to respect scene physics, or require additional annotations. In low-data domains like underwater environmental monitoring, the need for additional annotations is often the core bottleneck. Collecting new footage is hard, but labeling this dataset is even more time-consuming and costly, which limits how quickly a usable dataset can be constructed. We propose a unified fully automated zero-annotation pipeline that inserts class-conditioned trash objects into real underwater frames while preserving the original background context. Starting from sampled bounding boxes, each box is converted into an insertion mask and the object is pasted or generated within it, while the same box coordinates are automatically written as the corresponding annotation. Because labels are derived directly from the sampled boxes, the pipeline generates fully labeled training data for low-data settings without any manual annotation. Within this single pipeline we compare three strategies: copy-paste, Stable Diffusion inpainting, and DALL-E image-conditioned generation. We evaluate bounding-box faithfulness, visual realism, and the impact of the generated samples on downstream detector performance on the SeaClear underwater debris dataset. Results show that DALL-E improves detection performance in four of five domains, with relative gains of up to 96 percent over the real-only baseline.

</details>

Abstract: Global canopy height maps are increasingly used to estimate forest carbon, yet their regional accuracy is rarely tested. We present wall-to-wall maps of canopy height (10~m) and aboveground carbon density (30~m) for the Brazilian Atlantic Forest in 2024. Height was predicted by a deep learning network fusing Sentinel-2 reflectance, Sentinel-1 and ALOS PALSAR backscatter and ALOS elevation, trained on 7{,}179 and tested on 1{,}793 patches (58{,}800~km$^2$) from a statewide airborne LiDAR campaign over São Paulo state, Brazil. On held-out test patches, the root mean square error is 3.05~m at 10~m and 2.58~m at 30~m, and the model was validated at two LiDAR sites outside São Paulo. Over the same pixels, the Meta and World Resources Institute global product has more than twice this error. A power law fitted to 124 inventory plots converted canopy height to above ground carbon density. The remaining forest holds 1{,}944~Tg~C over 28.3~Mha. Global products therefore need local validation before use in carbon accounting.

### [269] [When Anchors Shift the Target: Advantage-Gated Adaptation for Diffusion Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23038)
*Pu Cao,Min Zhou,Ying Shu,Tiezheng Ge,Qing Song,Lu Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

Abstract: Forward-process reinforcement learning adapts diffusion and flow-matching models by re-noising rewarded generations into regression targets. Because negative advantages induce concave updates with unbounded repulsive gradients, existing methods stabilize training using cached predictions from a moving policy copy, yet these anchors also alter what positive feedback teaches the model to fit. We formalize this conflict as anchor coupling: after separating frozen-reference regularizers, affine moving-anchor rules cannot eliminate negative curvature while preserving every positive regression target. We propose Advantage-Gated Adaptation (AGA) to decouple these roles. Positive samples retain standard target fitting, whose self-limiting update vanishes at the observed target independently of the moving anchor. Negative samples instead use the first-order tangent of the concave objective at the moving-policy prediction, preserving the local repulsion direction with a prediction-independent gradient. Within the quadratic-anchor family, these requirements uniquely determine the parameter-free gate $ω(a)=\max(-a,0)$. Because the moving copy provides local linearization rather than retention, AGA assigns long-horizon behavior preservation to a frozen pretrained reference regularized at its operating classifier-free guidance scale, interpreted through an implicit-reward lens. Across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment, our experiments show that displacing positive targets degrades visual quality and heightens sensitivity to policy-copy schedules, whereas AGA consistently improves the reward--quality trade-off.

</details>


### [270] [Refin3R: Geometry-Guided Refinement for Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23051)
*Jongmin Lee,Jaeyeong Kim,Seungryong Kim*

Abstract: Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models predict dense point maps from multiple images in a single pass. Without explicit geometric constraints, however, their predictions are often inconsistent across views and lack fine-grained precision. Recent methods incorporate geometric priors such as camera pose or depth, but use them merely as auxiliary inputs, process each view independently, or refine the geometry only where the priors are available. We present Refin3R, which refines feed-forward point maps with Structure-from-Motion (SfM) points, propagating SfM geometry over the image grid. In its dual-branch architecture, a 2D branch extends SfM geometry beyond its coverage using geometry-aware backbone features, while a 3D branch reasons over the SfM points of all views, capturing multi-view structure that the image grid alone cannot represent. The two branches exchange information bidirectionally to jointly refine each view, yielding dense and accurate point maps. Across ScanNet++, ETH3D, Tanks\&Temples, and out-of-domain human and surgical scenes, Refin3R consistently outperforms feed-forward and geometry-guided methods in point map accuracy while running faster than the strongest geometry-guided baseline.

</details>

Abstract: Event-based cameras provide high temporal resolution, low power consumption, and a high dynamic range, but progress in event-based object detection remains constrained by the scarcity of annotated training data. To solve this issue, this paper introduces the tool UnrealEvent, a synthetic dataset-generation pipeline implemented in Unreal Engine 5.5. The pipeline combines a V2E-inspired event-frame generator with EasySynth semantic-rendering passes, allowing rendered level sequences to be converted into paired event representations and YOLO-format bounding-box annotations without manual labeling. Events are generated from per-pixel log-luminance differences between consecutive frames, with contrast thresholds sampled independently from a normal distribution to emulate the fabrication-induced threshold mismatch of real sensors. Using this pipeline, we provide a synthetic dataset and evaluate YOLOv8 detectors under synthetic, zero-shot real, and real fine-tuning training on eTraM. The results show strong in-domain synthetic performance but a substantial synthetic-to-real gap, especially for pedestrians. Fine-tuning on a small real subset improves real-domain performance, while the remaining gap highlights the importance of asset diversity and sensor calibration.

</details>

Abstract: Maritime search and rescue requires locating people in the water quickly, motivating the use of UAVs to survey the search area. During flight, camera motion and the longer exposures used in low light can blur small targets and hinder person detection. Event cameras record brightness changes as they occur, reducing the motion blur associated with longer exposure times. We investigate their use for maritime person detection by generating synthetic events using the v2e event simulator from public MOBDrone rescue videos and retaining the existing annotations. We compare the recurrent vision transformer model RVT-T with YOLOv8n and RT-DETR-R18 trained on exposure-matched images. On 843 test samples from held-out recordings, RVT-T achieves 15.63% person AP50:95, compared with 6.89% for YOLOv8n and 9.44% for RT-DETR-R18 under 100 ms synthetic exposure. Increasing exposure times from 50 to 200 ms progressively reduces frame-based detection accuracy, widening the gap to the event model. To evaluate the feasibility of on-board processing, we deploy our detector on a Jetson Orin Nano platform. The RVT-T model retains its accuracy and requires approximately 136 ms and 2.02 J per sample, allowing real-time processing on the UAV.

</details>

Abstract: Audio-visual question answering on lightweight user devices requires balancing speed, memory usage, privacy, and communication. One approach is to run audio-visual encoders on a server and a lightweight language decoder on the user device, keeping questions local while offloading media encoding. However, transferring and processing the full multimodal representation remains costly. We propose a training-free, query-agnostic token pruning framework that constructs a compact, reusable memory before transmission. We define video spatio-temporal blocks and aligned audio windows as players and estimate their importance through Monte Carlo Shapley valuation. We use an embedding coverage objective that combines query-independent utility with cross-modal redundancy calibration and rewards unimodal diversity through marginal coverage gains. We retain the original token embeddings of high-scoring players under a limited token budget and transfer the resulting memory once for reuse across subsequent local questions. Experiments on Video-MME, Daily-Omni, and Video-Holmes using video-SALMONN 2+ (3B) and video-SALMONN 2 Pro (4B) show that our method achieves the best or tied-best QA accuracy among the compared compression methods across all evaluated settings. On Daily-Omni with video-SALMONN 2+ (3B) at 80\% pruning, our method improves accuracy by 12.86--14.62 percentage points over these methods while achieving the lowest device-side LLM prefill and decoding latency, with up to $1.92\times$ decoding speedup at comparable embedding memory usage.

</details>


### [274] [FMT: Lossless Image Compression in a Fixed Number of Parallel Steps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23108)
*Ammar Ali*

Main category: cs.CV

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Learned lossless codecs make smaller files than common classical codecs, yet the images we archive and train on still use classical codecs. A main reason is time: a learned codec takes seconds to decode a photo that a classical codec opens in milliseconds, and their faster variants give up most of their gain in size. We argue that much of this time is spent waiting, not computing: the network waits for the values it conditions on, and the arithmetic decoder reads them one at a time. We introduce FMT (Fixed-step Multiscale Tree), a lossless image format whose decoding order and entropy coder are designed together so that neither wait grows with the image. FMT stores an image as a coarse-to-fine tree whose steps hold independent values: a GPU predicts each step at once and decodes it in parallel rANS lanes that carry their states from step to step, so an image of any size decodes in a fixed number of sequential steps. On this tree we build a family of bit-exact codecs, from a rule with no network to large networks, and no codec we ran on the same GPU beats any of them on both size and speed. Without a network, FMT beats NVIDIA's HTJ2K on size and speed; with a large one, it decodes Kodak photos 9 to 61X faster than learned codecs we ran on the same A100, at the same size or smaller.

</details>

Abstract: Photographic color grading shapes the tone and color of a photograph to achieve a desired look. Text-driven photographic look transfer lets users specify these adjustments in natural language, with the goal of reproducing the requested look across photographs while preserving their content. However, prior research on text-driven color grading uses different datasets, text instructions, target definitions, and metrics. These differences make it difficult to compare how faithfully the methods reproduce the same look. To compare methods under a shared protocol, we introduce LUTBench. We collect 91 three-dimensional lookup tables (3D LUTs) and define one photographic look per LUT with a specified input conversion. Applying these looks to 5,000 MIT-Adobe FiveK photographs produces 455,000 input-target image pairs with reproducible target images. For each look, we use templates to turn measured tone and color changes on fixed color samples into candidate clauses. We use training image measurements to verify applicable clauses before composing the text description. The evaluation measures target fidelity, tests sensitivity to predefined input conversions, and separates generalization to unseen looks from reproduction of seen looks on new photographs. Across 16 methods, stronger text alignment does not guarantee target fidelity. However, most supervised variants improve fidelity on both seen and unseen looks.

</details>

Abstract: Event cameras offer high temporal resolution and low power consumption, making them promising for speech-related applications. These include automatic lip reading, audiovisual speech analysis, and human-robot interaction, particularly in challenging environments characterized by low illumination or high dynamic range. Event-based lip reading, speaker recognition, and voice activity detection, however, mostly operate on a cropped region of interest (ROI), by hand or by the dataset, and no event-based method supplies that ROI as a spatial region; existing event-based face analysis gives the mouth as landmarks, which presents a mismatch between application and localization. The present work predicts a dense heatmap over the lip region from the full event "frame". Events are encoded as a polarity-separated voxel grid and processed at every location by a two-pathway continuous-time recurrent network (CT-RNN) with learned, log-space bounded time constants acting on the real elapsed time of each bin: a stateless fast pathway and a stateful slow pathway, utilised for refining and anchoring the prediction. The model reaches 69.5% on lips on four unseen speakers and switches target only 2.1 times per second, against 8.9 and 11.0 for convolutional baselines with
  more parameters. Adding a spatial encoder improves precision but fails on a new face scale. On a real event sensor, trained only on simulated events, the proposed model reaches 58.0% on lips against 43.2% for the CNN, with fewer than half its target switches.

</details>

Abstract: Visual foundation models are central to physical intelligence, yet their development remains constrained by fragmented training datasets, inconsistent annotation quality, and limited multimodal supervision. To address these challenges, we introduce GameRealm, a large-scale, richly annotated dataset that grounds visual observations in synchronized, game-native supervision. GameRealm encompasses over 120 million RGB frames and 1,000 hours of gameplay footage across all views. Beyond its unprecedented scale, GameRealm provides rich multimodal supervision, including metric depth, camera parameters, synchronized audio, control inputs, and 3D point trajectories. Its diversity further extends to flexible view configurations, spanning single-view, stereo, multi-view, and panoramic recordings. To enable scalable acquisition across diverse game environments, we develop a unified data collection pipeline that combines human-operated interaction with agentic exploration. Extensive experiments across diverse visual learning tasks demonstrate that GameRealm consistently strengthens visual foundation models in both visual generation and geometric learning. These results establish GameRealm as a versatile and valuable data resource, unlocking new opportunities for advancing visual intelligence.

</details>

Abstract: $π^4$ is a unified foundation model for 4D scene perception. From diverse, uncalibrated RGB observations in the wild---a single image, an unordered collection, or a video sequence---it recovers dense geometry, camera poses, and metric scale, together with 2D/3D point tracks and moving-object masks for multi-image inputs. It couples a geometry branch with a geometry-conditioned motion branch, which predicts image-space correspondences that are lifted into 3D using reconstructed target-view geometry. Across diverse benchmarks, $π^4$ achieves the best overall average rank in geometry evaluation, alongside competitive performance in single- and multiview 2D/3D point tracking and moving-object segmentation. With a shared backbone, the full geometry-and-motion system contains approximately 0.5B parameters---less than half the size of most baselines. It produces joint reconstructions and tracks in one forward pass, including large-scale scenes with thousands of input images despite training with contexts of at most 64 views. We further present the data and training recipe underlying these capabilities, together with empirical studies of key design choices. Code and models are available at https://github.com/yyfz/Pi4.

</details>

Abstract: Video question answering under tight frame budgets requires preserving localized motion and event order, yet current video multimodal large language models (MLLMs) reduce comprehension to static multi-image reasoning by discarding unselected frames and encoding keyframes independently: (1) Spatially, whole-frame pooling dilutes small moving targets into static backgrounds; (2) Temporally, dropping intermediate frames or averaging tokens erases inter-frame motion, confusing direction, speed, and order. We propose VIVID (Visual Inter-frame Variations and Individual Dynamics), which moves beyond static snapshots like human vision: keyframes capture core states while inter-frame Patch Diffs preserve neighboring changes within $K_f$ keyframes. Autonomous Delta-Driven Keyframe Planning (ADKP) selects anchors from a $3\text{ fps}$ pool and feeds surrounding patch differences into Dual-Road Change Memory (DRCM), forming Dynamic Tokens at zero extra anchor tokens alongside a camera-stabilized Active Motion Region of Interest (ROI) and chronological Entity State Chains. Adding $0.22\%$ trainable parameters to a frozen $27\text{B}$ backbone, VIVID-Small ($50$ examples, $\sim 1.5$ GPU hours) beats all eleven baselines in overall accuracy across $8,083$ questions and trails only VIVID-Large ($3,600$ examples), which leads on MotionBench, Video-MME, and TempCompass.

</details>

Abstract: A working-memory model must remember stimuli and use its memory flexibly to perform different tasks. Here we introduce a model of working memory for images that combines dynamic activity with temporary associations between image features. We train the model on five tasks, including remembering a sample while other images are presented, categorizing distractor images while remembering the sample, and determining whether an image is new or has been presented before. The same trained model performs all tasks, guided by explicit cues about which response is required. We use this flexible memory model to ask how delay, distraction, and image history affect performance. Delays have little effect on sample-memory performance for the delays we tested, while seeing more images leads to more false alarms even when the target image appeared equally recently in all cases. On the sample-memory-plus-categorization task, the probability of correct responses on all questions decreases as more questions are asked, despite robust sample-memory performance. Finally, the model maintains stable performance across 40 brief trials of each task under the tested conditions. Together, these results demonstrate how a single working-memory system can be flexibly used to perform different tasks, and how its errors depend on both previous images and task demands.

</details>


### [281] [EviSlide-MIL: Multi-Query Pooling and Shared Prediction for Class-Incremental Whole-Slide Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23212)

Abstract: Histopathological analysis of whole-slide images (WSIs) plays a critical role in cancer diagnosis, and multiple instance learning (MIL) is widely adopted for WSI analysis under weak supervision. As new tumor subtypes are introduced, extending existing MIL models requires learning new classes while retaining knowledge of previously learned ones. This presents a class-incremental learning challenge, where models must acquire new classes with limited access to historical training data and recognize all learned classes without task identifiers at inference. A major obstacle is forgetting, as updates to feature aggregation and prediction modules can degrade performance on previously learned classes. To address this challenge, we propose \textbf{EviSlide-MIL}, a class-incremental WSI framework that integrates multiple learnable queries per class with a shared prediction module. The queries aggregate frozen patch features into class-specific representations, while the shared prediction module limits parameter growth across incremental stages. By freezing queries associated with previously learned classes, EviSlide-MIL preserves their attention patterns for identical inputs, while experience replay and knowledge distillation further mitigate forgetting as shared parameters are updated. Experiments on eight tumor subtypes from The Cancer Genome Atlas demonstrate that EviSlide-MIL achieves improved performance and reduced forgetting compared with competing methods across most learning settings, demonstrating its potential for scalable and knowledge-preserving WSI classification. Code and pretrained models will be released.

</details>


### [282] [Learning Grounded Reasoning from Human Gaze](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23209)

Abstract: Human visual reasoning is driven by systematic exploration and extraction of task-relevant visual evidence. Human gaze provides a window into this process, making gaze a useful source of guidance for visually-grounded reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs). However, human gaze also includes noise and redundancies that may transfer poorly to VLMs. In this work, we investigate how human gaze data can be effectively leveraged to improve visual reasoning in VLMs, while maintaining alignment with human behavior. We collect 7,109 human gaze trials from a diverse and challenging set of visual reasoning tasks, and train grounded reasoning models with different forms of gaze guidance. Across evaluations in chart understanding, visual search, and counting, we find that using human gaze as a prior for grounded reasoning consistently outperforms supervision using the full gaze sequence. Based on these findings, we introduce GGR (Gaze-Guided Reasoner), which achieves greater human alignment and more reliable visual grounding while matching or outperforming leading grounded-reasoning baselines in accuracy. Reinforcement learning further improves task accuracy, gaze alignment, and grounding quality despite using no gaze supervision. Behavioral analyses show that gaze supervised models exhibit more visual exploration before arriving at an answer. Our results suggest that the value of human gaze lies in guiding spatial exploration and shaping reasoning behavior without requiring complete imitation of human behavior.

</details>

Abstract: Abstracting structured buildings from limited observations is essential for 3D urban modeling and digital twins. Partial point clouds and overhead images of the same building provide incomplete 3D surface measurements and visual cues to its footprint, roof geometry, and rooftop structures, respectively. This motivates a unified framework that aligns geometric and visual cues in 3D and reconstructs buildings from either or both modalities. Building on this connection, we present Any2Building, a unified model for Level of Detail 2 (LoD2) building reconstruction from partial point clouds, overhead images, or both, with optional coarse geometric priors. At its core is an occupancy-grounded shared spatial queries representation that organizes available observations within a common 3D grid. Supervised by complete building surface voxels, these queries connect observational evidence to target geometry, enabling a shared generator to learn structural priors across tasks and providing spatial guidance for recovering missing structure. A trained voxel conditional surface-generation and low-poly remeshing pipeline then converts the predicted structure into compact LoD2 building models. To support this framework, we introduce BuildingWorld-Image, comprising approximately one million paired samples of partial point clouds, overhead images, and LoD2 building models across 18 cities. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that Any2Building outperforms existing methods in geometric reconstruction accuracy across unimodal and multimodal point-cloud completion, single-image building generation, and prior-assisted generation, while maintaining detailed and robust recovery when point cloud input modality is degraded.

</details>

Abstract: Applications such as visual accessibility agents and robotics require answering questions about recent video content promptly, over a bounded window of past frames (e.g., the last two minutes). Hybrid vision-language models (VLMs), which interleave attention with linear recurrent layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), are attractive for this setting, but maintaining a sliding window is expensive. The reason is that recurrent layers combine all past frames into a shared state, so expiring old frames normally requires re-running the entire window through the model. We observe that the recurrent update is affine in the state with input-dependent coefficients, so the effect of each frame can be captured as a transformation that composes associatively. We present Spool, a training-free framework that caches per-frame state transformations and composes them efficiently as the window advances, removing expired frames from each recurrent layer's state without replaying frames through the backbone. Removal is exact within each layer; as with key-value eviction in attention, expired frames can retain residual influence through the cached inputs computed by earlier layers. We design a dedicated data structure and GPU kernels to achieve low caching and composition overhead. Across five open-source hybrid VLMs and four video reasoning benchmarks, Spool achieves the lowest p95 time-to-first-token in all 20 model-benchmark tests, reducing it by a geometric mean of $6.6\times$ over the faster of two recent vLLM baselines, while improving accuracy by 2.3 percentage points on average.

</details>

Abstract: Autoregressive video diffusion models generate frames based on new observations and inputs, making them a promising medium for interactive visual experiences. However, existing models are orders of magnitude too slow for fluid interaction. This paper introduces FastFrames, a family of fast autoregressive video diffusion models that achieves interactive latency without compromising quality. We profile the inference pipeline of state-of-the-art models and find that operations often treated as fixed implementation details, such as VAE decoding and KV cache updates, in fact account for over half of end-to-end inference latency. We challenge the assumption that these components are indispensable and replace them with lightweight alternatives. Our 1.3B variant runs at 36 ms per frame, or 28 responses per second (RPS), on a single B200 GPU while matching the generation quality of a state-of-the-art model that is nearly 8x slower. Our 14B variant runs at 45 ms per frame, or 22 RPS, on an 8x B200 GPU node and yields significantly better visual quality and realism. We show that FastFrames enables real-time interaction in applications such as camera-conditioned and physics-conditioned video generation.

</details>

Abstract: We present Limite 1B - Violetto, a one-billion-parameter model designed for high-throughput mathematical reasoning. Limite is trained from scratch on 237 billion tokens from a carefully curated, mathematics-centric corpus and supports context lengths of up to 131k tokens. Its architecture and training recipe were developed together to maximize reasoning capability while keeping training and inference costs low. Post-training combines knowledge distillation, targeted supervised fine-tuning, and RLVR on a curated curriculum. Limite achieves 94.01% on AIME 2026 and 74.25% on BeyondAIME, placing it among models many times its size on our evaluation suite. The model is deliberately only lightly instruction-tuned: it is intended primarily as a single-turn mathematical solver and as a fast, parallel component of larger systems, rather than as a general-purpose assistant. We release the model, its base and value checkpoints, and an optimized serving implementation under the Apache 2.0 license.

</details>

Abstract: Background: Crisis helplines are essential to mental health systems but are often understaffed and underfunded. LLMs could support counselors, but existing benchmarks do not capture the escalation, risk assessment, and de-escalation central to crisis conversations, while privacy restrictions limit use of real transcripts.
Methods: We present CrisisBench, a privacy-preserving suite for single- and multi-turn crisis counseling evaluation. It includes 1,085 single-turn tasks from 200 de-identified conversations and 1,185 patient LLMs derived from hotline metadata. Patient models and a 23-item LLM judge were calibrated against expert counselors.
Results: We evaluated five GPT models plus draft-then-edit and retrieval-augmented generation. All systems underperformed human counselors on excessive empathy and questioning in single-turn evaluation. The rubric achieved human-human Krippendorff's $α$ = 0.51 and LLM-human $α$ = 0.54 (Pearson r = 0.80). In multi-turn evaluation, retrieval-augmented generation scored highest overall (0.961), with all systems within 0.058. Microskills and style/tone were near ceiling, while productivity and safety/risk accounted for most between-system variation. Counselors rated patient LLMs realistic (86%) and useful (92%).
Conclusion: CrisisBench enables scalable, privacy-preserving evaluation of crisis counseling. Current systems perform strongly on core structural competencies but still diverge from experts on important response-level failure modes. We release the rubrics, judge prompts, and evaluation tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Estimating full-night electroencephalography (EEG) spectrograms from peripheral measurements could extend EEG-informed sleep analysis to recordings acquired without EEG. This task requires compact representations of signals with different temporal structures. We introduce Sleep2Word, a framework for translating respiration, electrocardiography (ECG), or photoplethysmography (PPG) into EEG spectrograms through duration-aware physiological words. Building on context-dependent chunking, Sleep2Word groups physiological embeddings into variable-duration chunks and jointly quantizes their content and duration into modality-specific wordbooks. A block-autoregressive translator then predicts target EEG word identities and occurrence-specific duration refinements within fixed-duration blocks. This design accommodates independent source and target wordbooks, limits temporal drift, and shortens the autoregressive decoding path. Trained on over 20K PSG cases across 9 datasets, Sleep2Word consistently outperforms existing spectrogram generation baselines in EEG spectrogram reconstruction, sleep staging, arousal detection, and survival prediction. Compressing full night sequences by up to 69.7%, the learned words yield a favorable accuracy-compute trade-off. Sleep event analysis further shows that the learned words capture physiologically plausible associations across modalities, suggesting their potential for interpretable cross-modal analysis.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) excel in vision-language tasks; yet, whether they maintain consistent understanding of information-rich visuals (InfoVis) when the surface language changes remains unclear. To quantify this capability, we introduce MInfoVisQA, a parallel benchmark with 8,960 QA configurations derived from 128 seed instances across 24 languages. We construct seed-matched, language-parallel visuals and QA pairs by disentangling numerical-structural data from text and programmatically synchronizing translated labels. We evaluate 25 MLLMs under two settings: Language-Aligned QA (LQA, matching visual-text languages) and Cross-Language QA (XQA, fixed QA language with varying visual languages). The results show that aggregate accuracy masks substantial robustness gaps: two models with comparable mean LQA accuracy (80.4\%) exhibit performance spreads of 7.8 and 20.3 percentage points across languages. Moreover, all 25 models score lower with non-English visuals under fixed English QA, underscoring the need for cross-language sensitivity metrics.

</details>

Abstract: Deployed LLMs field hundreds of millions of conversations a day and refuse a non-trivial fraction of user requests, yet the user-side cost of refusal, whether users disengage and by how much, is unmeasured on any production model: a blind spot for the consumer-facing platforms built on them. A/B-testing refusal is ethically and operationally constrained on frontier-lab products and unavailable to third-party platforms deploying these models through a fixed API. We introduce RAW, a method that uses LLM stochasticity as a natural experiment: at near-identical prompts in any existing log, the same model sometimes refuses and sometimes does not, and that variation identifies the causal user-side effect of refusal. Applying the method to 15 deployed LLMs in LMSYS-Chat-1M and WildChat, with user-clustered inference, we find that for about half of the models we can measure precisely, a refusal costs 3 to 17 pp of subsequent re-engagement; the rest show no distinguishable effect, among them one deployment whose positive point estimate we trace to persistent users retrying the refused request. Our contribution is the measurement method, which runs on any deployed model from logs alone for about \$0.30 per 1,000 conversations. We release labels, moderation scores, prompt embeddings, and pipeline code.

</details>

Abstract: Generative AI agents can help journalists explore large document collections. But exploratory workflows can require substantial token use and time, especially when the task is broad and the relevant subquestions are not yet known. This paper introduces FieldGuide, an agent-facing corpus map that organizes a document collection into a topic hierarchy and exposes tools for corpus orientation, scoped search, and bounded reading. I evaluate FieldGuide on five journalistic document-exploration tasks using 38 Chicago Police Department public-records documents, comparing it with raw-file access and retrieval-augmented generation. Across the five tasks, FieldGuide used 27% fewer tokens and 41% less elapsed time than raw-file access; relative to RAG, it used 38% fewer tokens and 21% less elapsed time. An accuracy audit identified four minor issues in FieldGuide's responses, comparable to the other conditions. In blind ratings of generated reporting leads, FieldGuide's mean ratings were higher than RAG's and lower than the raw-file condition's. These descriptive results suggest that corpus orientation may reduce runtime token use and elapsed time without severe accuracy failures. They also motivate further study of whether selective access limits broad, cross-document discovery.

</details>


### [292] [Can an LLM dream? Testing the overfitted brain hypothesis on an agent with text memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21648)
*Eric Zou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>

Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Hoel (2021) proposed that dreams distort replayed experience so that the brain does not overfit to recent events. We test this hypothesis on an LLM agent (Claude Opus 5.5) that stores what it learns in a text memory and rewrites that memory each night from the day's conversation transcript. The agent goes through ten simulated days of conversation built from LongMemEval. We corrupt 5, 10, 20 or 40 percent of the transcript's turns before each nightly rewrite in one of two ways. Detail corruption changes one or two details in a turn to plausible wrong ones, or replaces the turn with one by the same speaker from an earlier day. Random-text corruption replaces half of the selected turns with a passage of Moby-Dick and inserts Moby-Dick words into the other half. A size limit on the memory, rising from 19,500 to 117,000 tokens over ten nights, set from human free-recall data, forces it to forget. We learned that corruption did not help the agent apply what it learned in earlier conversations to new tasks in any of 30 runs (ten conditions, three different conversation histories). Replacing details in 40 percent of turns lowered recall from 76 to 60 percent. Random text had no consistent effect on recall, with a paired difference of 0 $\pm$ 2 points at 40 percent, because the model recognized it as corrupted and left it out of memory. Replaced details were stored as fact even when another turn held the correct value, and the agent answered 40 percent of those questions correctly, compared with 75 percent without corruption. These results suggest that a pretrained model can separate corrupted text from real events, so a fair test needs a learner that cannot make this separation as easily, such as a model fine-tuned on the corrupted days or an agent whose days take place in an invented world.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, we propose an alternative approach to human evaluation of literary fictions by employing LLMs themselves as evaluators within the GrAImes framework. We apply this methodology to assess human-written and AI-generated microfictions in Spanish, to five PhD professors in literature and sixteen literary enthusiasts, and to short stories in both Spanish and English. By comparing the evaluations performed by LLMs with those of human judges, we examine the degree of alignment and divergence between both perspectives, thereby assessing the feasibility of LLMs as auxiliary literary evaluators. Our analysis focuses on the alignment of responses from LLMs with those of human evaluators, providing insights into the potential of LLMs in literary assessment. The conducted experiments reveal that while LLMs cannot be regarded as substitutes for human judges in the evaluation of literary microfictions and short stories, with a Krippendorff's alpha reliability coefficient less than 0.66, they can serve as a valuable tool that offers an initial perspective on the editorial quality of the texts in question. Overall, this study contributes to the ongoing discourse on the role of artificial intelligence in literature, underlining both its methodological constraints and its potential as a complementary resource for literary evaluation.

</details>


### [294] [Consistency Is Not Correction: Separating Confidence, Abstention, and Recovery in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21651)
*Khushal Sethi*

Abstract: A controller that resamples an answer, escalates to a larger model, or abstains is usually justified by a confidence signal. Three claims about such a signal do not follow from one another: it ranks answers (discrimination), it supports withholding some of them (selective prediction), and it changes an answer for the better (correction utility). We give an exact decomposition of the third into gated recoveries minus gated harms, which bounds whether a comparison can succeed before it is run: a gate that only replaces a proposal can act only where the base policy was already wrong, so the initial error count caps the discordant pairs any correct gate can produce. When that cap falls below the count a paired test needs, the experiment cannot return a positive result for a working mechanism, nor distinguish one from a gate that never fired. We measure all three targets on two model scales and find the bound binds. In a small-model suite a perturbation-agreement verifier reaches within-policy AUROC 0.990 (95% interval [0.95, 1.00]) while every episode outcome matches single-sample decoding at 4.32 times the call count. In a controlled 840-episode study no verifier replaced the proposal on any of the 60 GSM8K decisions, so that comparison tested nothing; the entire GSM8K gain across six controllers is one task, and it is the task whose identical temperature-zero repeats disagreed, making it the one answer the service does not reproduce. On the diagnostic set the gate could reach four errors while the test needed six, so even a perfect gate had no significant result available. We report two checks that would have caught this before any budget was spent, and show that tie handling in rankings, tied selection cutoffs, and unequal output limits each move a headline number in the same dataset. The binding constraint on this literature is measurement resolution, not the choice of signal.

</details>


### [295] [From Preference Data to Personalization: Tracing Sycophancy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21650)

Abstract: Sycophancy in large language models is often measured as agreement with the user, yet agreement can be appropriate when the user's claim is valid, their preference is genuine, or their experience warrants acknowledgment. We study sycophancy as inappropriate agreement: validating a claim, preference, or self-assessment when the context calls for correction, qualification, or resistance. We introduce a validity-aware sycophancy grader and SycoDrift, a persona-targeted evaluation set of 3,298 validity-annotated probes with controlled annotations for user openness and stakes. We use this framework to analyze three sources of agreement pressure: human preference data, automated judge specification, and in-context personalization. First, preference for validating responses in human datasets (Chatbot Arena, HH-RLHF, Community Alignment) is weak and concentrated where agreement is often appropriate. Second, in a selected-value, single-model experiment, emphasized judge rubrics produce consistent but modest increases relative to paired suppressed rubrics, demonstrating a plausible mechanism for training-time amplification. Third, in off-policy evaluations across 14 models, inference-time personalization increases inappropriate agreement, with the largest effect from matched user profiles placed directly before the query.

</details>

Abstract: Financial sentiment analysis is critical for automated trading, portfolio management, and risk assessment, heavily relying on models like FinBERT. Despite high benchmark accuracy, these models remain vulnerable to subtle adversarial text modifications, threatening the integrity of algorithmic decision-making. In this paper, we propose Agent-driven Semantic Inversion (ASI), a novel adversarial attack framework targeting financial sentiment models through domain-specific semantic manipulation. Utilizing an LLM-powered multi-agent pipeline, our approach identifies eligible financial news sentences and applies targeted lexical substitutions. We empirically evaluate ASI against FinBERT, one of the most widely deployed financial sentiment models. Our results reveal a striking vulnerability: the attack successfully deceived FinBERT in 90.85% of eligible cases, with the model frequently maintaining its original sentiment prediction despite the fundamental inversion of financial performance indicators. Through qualitative analysis and token-level investigation, we demonstrate that FinBERT critically over-relies on contextual keywords and syntactic patterns rather than true logical comprehension of financial semantics. Our findings highlight the urgent need for logic-aware, robust financial NLP models to ensure the reliability of automated decision-making systems.

</details>


### [297] [Token Commitment as a Control Surface for Steering Masked Diffusion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21669)

Abstract: Masked diffusion models repeatedly predict provisional tokens before a sampler decides which predictions become permanent. Existing inference-time steering methods primarily modify activations, logits, or denoising trajectories, while treating this commitment policy as a fixed decoding detail. We instead study token commitment as a diffusion-specific control surface and separate it into two decisions: which tokens to commit and how many tokens to commit at each step. For the first, we introduce counterfactual target-aware commitment, which ranks unresolved positions by a mixture of model confidence and leave-one-token-out target contribution. For the second, we introduce calibrated adaptive budgets driven by target progress and confidence. Across language and vision-language settings in the LLaDA family, multiple targets, two activation-steering methods, and an unsteered control, both mechanisms yield empirically monotonic target-quality trade-offs in every configuration we evaluate. Adaptive schedules also recover competitive operating points relative to scorer-free stochastic schedules matched in average denoising cost. Because both mechanisms require only a scalar score evaluated on a provisional completion, they apply unchanged when the target is an automatic proxy for task success rather than an output attribute: NLI entailment for dialogue summarization and CLIPScore for captioning, neither of which corresponds to a single reusable steering direction. We reproduce the behavior of both mechanisms on a second masked-diffusion family, Dream-7B, for one control target and one task-success target. These results characterize commitment--which provisional predictions become irreversible, and how rapidly--as an interpretable control surface for steering masked diffusion generation under the conditions we test.

</details>

Abstract: Interpretability methods can locate the attention heads and sparse autoencoder (SAE) features that change the answer a language model gives to a moral prompt, e.g., whether "Stealing medicine for a dying child is morally" continues with "justified" or "wrong". Such a component could respond to moral content, to the judgment of conduct by any norm, or to the word "morally" in the prompt. We built 227 minimal pairs of short phrases, validated by five annotators, whose two versions differ in a moral standard, in another kind of norm (prudence, etiquette, aesthetics or evidence) or in a descriptive quality, and showed them to 14 open-weight models (Llama, Gemma and Qwen, 1B to 14B, base and instruction-tuned), with and without the word "morally". In every model, the heads that change the moral answer belong to an evaluation circuit the models use for every kind of normative judgment we tested: these heads carry the same share of the effect for moral as for other normative pairs (pooled ratio 1.00, 97.5% confidence interval [0.92, 1.07], with the word; 0.90, [0.84, 0.96], without it), and the models need them as much for both. The models need these heads also when nothing in the prompt asks for a judgment, and on 400 scenarios of the ETHICS benchmark, where removing them takes away 37 to 39% of the difference between acceptable and unacceptable scenarios, against 1 to 3% for ten random heads. The SAE features we located respond mostly to the word "morally" rather than to what the phrase is about. The models represent the category of a pair (the standard the annotators assigned to it): it can be read from their hidden states, and, in the eight models we tested, removing the direction that carries it weakens the moral verdict by a few percent, and more than the other verdicts in seven of them. We release the pairs, the ratings, the code, the trained SAE dictionaries and the result files.

</details>

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes decision-making contexts, where interactions among components can introduce fairness risks that remain undetected when auditing components or collective outcomes in isolation. As LLM-based multi-agent systems become more prevalent, understanding how biased information propagates through agent interactions is essential for assessing their fairness. We introduce a controlled bias injection framework that replaces selected agent responses with predefined biased ones, enabling an intervention-based analysis of how network topology and injection configurations shape system behavior. We propose decision divergence metrics that quantify the effects of these interventions at the agent, network, and system levels, and evaluate five topologies on three binary classification tasks using LLMs from the Qwen and Gemma families. Sequential networks show the greatest divergence, whereas fully connected and mesh networks show less. Injection position also matters: in star networks, injecting the central agent has larger effects than injecting peripheral agents. Models further differ in whether divergence follows the injected bias. These findings highlight the role of network structure in the propagation of biased information and motivate topology-aware auditing of fairness risks in multi-agent systems.

</details>

Abstract: Realistic user simulation enables the study of human interactions when direct experimentation is costly or potentially harmful. However, existing simulators largely overlook the psychological mechanisms that drive user behavior. We introduce SimAI, a framework for mechanistic user simulation that models how psychological factors interact to shape user responses. To overcome the challenge of supervising open-ended behavior, we propose a graph reconstruction objective that provides graph-level rewards based on how well simulated conversations reflect the underlying psychological relationships. To support this approach, we construct PSYCHE, a dataset of 18,576 profile-network pairs derived from 516 psychotherapy cases. Automatic evaluations show that SimAI achieves greater simulation and behavioral diversity than existing patient simulators while maintaining strong profile alignment. In an interactive study with 30 mental-health professionals and trainees, SimAI receives the highest average ratings for conversational authenticity, realistic help-seeking behavior, and clinical training utility. Beyond simulation quality, SimAI enables realistic clinical training and uncovers safety failures in prolonged AI interactions with vulnerable users. Code is available at https://github.com/behavioral-data/ANGEL-UserSim.

</details>

Abstract: Evaluating retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in Persian law remains challenging because benchmarks lack realism, grounding, and coverage of legal reasoning. Existing resources are either synthetically generated from statutory texts, lacking natural information needs, or derived from informal legal question--answer sources without explicit linkage to authoritative legal documents, making them unsuitable for rigorous evaluation of grounded legal reasoning. To address this gap, we introduce TUSI-Bench, a structured Persian legal benchmark designed for the evaluation of RAG-based systems. TUSI-Bench is built upon a multi-layered legal knowledge framework containing 7,261 legal articles and integrating codified statutes, legal commentaries, doctrinal writings, and Supreme Court unification rulings. We also construct a legal knowledge graph linking articles and concepts for structure-aware retrieval. Based on this foundation, TUSI-Bench provides two complementary evaluation tasks: (i) TUSI-QA, an open-ended question answering benchmark containing 1,999 questions that requires grounded responses with explicit legal citations, and (ii) TUSI-Bar, a multiple-choice benchmark containing approximately 500 four-option questions derived from Iranian Bar examination questions. Furthermore, we propose a graph-based retrieval baseline that leverages relational structure within the legal knowledge graph. Experimental results demonstrate that incorporating graph-based relations improves retrieval recall by up to 8 percentage points compared to standard baselines, highlighting the importance of structured legal representations for RAG systems. The proposed benchmark provides a standardized resource for evaluating grounded legal reasoning in Persian and enables future research on structured legal intelligence.

</details>

Abstract: Language models are increasingly asked where real actors stand: what a party's platform prioritises, what an institution has committed to. Such judgments are gradable, carrying both an order and a magnitude. Nearly every benchmark that tests them asks about one entity at a time, then reads that score as the model's knowledge of the domain. Order and magnitude, however, need not be acquired together. Text about social entities carries far more comparison than measurement, so a model may hold a dependable ordering with no anchored scale beneath it. We ask whether a model can place a documented social position on a scale or only order the actors it compares, and what that means for how such knowledge is measured. We test this on party positions in Japan and South Korea, where the Comparative Manifesto Project's coded data supplies a rare external answer key. Asked to order four parties, models beat the strongest fixed-answer baseline by more than a factor of two; asked to place one party on the same scale, they fall below it. The ordering survives inside those failed placements, and supplying the coded values lifts placement to near ceiling. Comparative and absolute questions about the same fact therefore measure different things here, and for party issue salience a benchmark built from one cannot speak for the other.

</details>


### [303] [Call-Efficient ICD-10 Code Prediction via Supervised Candidate Generation and Selective LLM Invocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21891)

Abstract: Automatic assignment of ICD-10 codes from short clinical diagnosis text is a practical medical text classification and normalization task. The task is difficult because diagnosis phrases are short and noisy, the ICD-10 label space is hierarchical, and the class distribution is strongly imbalanced. This paper presents a call-efficient ICD-10 code prediction pipeline that combines transformer embeddings, tree-based supervised candidate generation, candidate-level reranking, and a gated LLM assistance. The evaluation uses a leakage-safe validation protocol: model and threshold selection are based on out-of-fold cross-validation predictions, while final reporting is performed on a locked test set with no shared source-file groups. The supervised conditional parent-code candidate generator reaches 74.32% Top-1 accuracy on the locked test set and 93.05% Top-10 candidate coverage. A direct zero-shot GPT-5.6-Sol baseline reaches 81.01% Top-1 accuracy, but requires an invocation of this large model for every diagnosis. The final deployable system, Pre-LLM fusion, reaches 81.24% OOF-CV accuracy and 83.19% locked-test accuracy while calling the LLM for only 31.97% of cases. This improves the locked-test error rate from 25.68% for the supervised parent-code baseline to 16.81%, corresponding to a 34.5% relative error reduction. The results show that the most effective role of the LLM is controlled reranking on uncertain examples rather than unrestricted coding or full-dataset invocation.

</details>

Abstract: Controlled studies of latent two-hop reasoning train transformers to predict only the answer, whereas language models are trained to predict every token. We ask whether a model trained on every token learns to answer two-hop questions whose intermediate entity appears in no two-hop training example. We build a synthetic corpus with such questions. On this corpus every term of the loss can be computed exactly. A looped transformer, a standard transformer and DiscoLoop answer almost none of these questions. We therefore introduce the Baton family of looped transformers. They pass the intermediate entity to the second loop as a token embedding. When trained to predict only the answer, two of its three variants answer these questions almost perfectly. We then relate the success and failure of each model to three design conditions. Experiments that change one condition at a time agree with this hypothesis. When Baton is trained on every token, however, its accuracy on these questions rises and then declines. We further split the gradient of the next-token loss into three terms. Removing the two terms that depend on the particular training set raises the peak accuracy but does not remove the decline. The decline arises in the second hop while the model still recalls both facts.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on medical question answering and diagnostic reasoning benchmarks. However, existing evaluations typically provide all patient information upfront and assess only the final diagnosis or rationale. This setup ignores the need to acquire diagnostic evidence incrementally from incomplete information in real-world diagnosis. To bridge this gap, we introduce DiagTrace, a source-grounded benchmark for evaluating whether LLMs can reconstruct the documented diagnostic trajectory through interactive information seeking. Each benchmark instance is built from a PubMed Central case report and includes an initial presentation, an event bank, a clinical course, and diagnostic references. DiagTrace contains 510 cases across eight specialties, with 225 used to evaluate four LLMs on clinical-course alignment, interaction quality, and efficiency. Results reveal trade-offs in coverage, source-consistent ordering, and turn use.

</details>

Abstract: Checking whether a generated sentence is supported by its source document is a standard safeguard for AI-written text, and the strongest published checkers are single models. We describe GauntletScore, an evidence engine whose few rules and thresholds were set on the benchmark's development split. It puts typed questions to independent readers from different providers and records each answer as a measurement in an evidence ledger. It combines the measurements with a fixed Bayesian rule that counts a contradiction only when two readers agree. On the full LLM-AggreFact test split (29,320 claims across 11 datasets), following the leaderboard's approach, GauntletScore achieves 79.51 balanced accuracy, above every average published on the leaderboard as of 15 November 2026. That places it level with the best of them, nimble-v4-max (79.45 from its published dataset scores; the engine's 95% interval, 78.47 to 80.53, contains it). On the same claims it beats two of its three readers used alone (Readers A and B) by 1.66 and 2.17 points (95% intervals 0.80 to 2.53 and 1.29 to 3.06). Without AggreFact-CNN, described below, the leads are 0.90 and 1.32 and both intervals stay above zero. The engine also records the evidence behind every verdict, which a single model does not. Its largest margin is on full-length news articles checked against multi-sentence summaries (AggreFact-CNN), a format that resembles much regulated writing. There it scores 76.45, the best on the leaderboard, against 70.1 for the next best and 63.9 to 67.8 for the six systems with the highest averages. The test split was run once, on an engine frozen before the first test claim, under a stopping plan committed in advance. We release per-claim predictions and the scoring script.

</details>


### [307] [RLDE: A Reinforcement Learning Framework for Interpretable Iterative Document Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22039)

Abstract: Improving the quality, clarity, and flow of long documents remains a challenging problem in NLP, as human editing typically occurs through iterative refinement rather than a single-pass rewrite. Most existing automated systems act as black-box generators or rely on supervised learning, limiting interpretability and failing to capture the sequential nature of revisions. In this paper, we propose RLDE, an automated end-to-end metric-driven RL framework that formulates document revision as a reference-free MDP problem (unsupervised with respect to paired document-revision targets). Working with a limited action space (NOOP, INSERT, DELETE, REPLACE), RLDE leverages Pointer Networks for dynamic span selection at the token level and sends generative actions to the frozen BART-base model to obtain context-aware suggestions. PPO agents learn to optimize a multi-objective reward function based on readability, perplexity, and grammar metrics with the help of a novel SBERT-based Semantic Preservation Penalty constraint to avoid deletion degeneracy. Evaluations on the BEA-2019 dataset showed statistically significant results (p < 0.01) across all metrics, where Flesch Reading Ease increased from 49.8 to 59.4, perplexity decreased from 63.7 to 39.8, while preserving 93% of semantic information. When compared to Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompt-engineered models like GPT-4 and LLaMA-2, RLDE shows high performance at a very low computational inference cost. Moreover, the quality of edits generated by RLDE has been verified via human evaluations, scoring 4.9/5 for meaning preservation and 4.4/5 for edit quality.

</details>

PLT achieved higher emergency severity index (ESI) accuracy (57.5% vs 42.8%, $P < 0.001$), lower undertriage (13.1% vs 29.8%, $P < 0.001$), and higher clinician ratings across all domains. rPLT produced shorter and more stable outputs with higher automated readability scores. SAFE-Triage identified trade-offs between acuity accuracy, undertriage risk, communication quality, readability, and summary quality, supporting pre-deployment refinement and ongoing monitoring by showing where triage tools are efficient, need improvement, or need clinician oversight.
