Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Coding agents built to repair software now write, test, and repair programs for robots, laboratories, and industrial controllers. Unlike in software engineering, no test suite comes with the code. Whether a program works must be established by running it on a robot, in a physics simulator, or through the policies it helps to train, and such evidence is costly to obtain and can itself be wrong. As these agents take on more physical tasks, how their programs are judged matters as much as how they are written. This paper examines this emerging area, agentic intelligence for the physical world, by treating the world as the program's test suite. We organize the discussion around three questions: how agents write programs for the physical world, how these programs are tested, and how agents learn from the results. We first trace how language models for robots and software engineering agents came together, ask which software testing practices carry over to physical tests, and describe each program by where it sits relative to the actuator and by what judges it. For writing, we cover programs that act as robot policies and programs that support policy learning by producing rewards, tasks, and training worlds. For testing, we examine what physical trials cost, what traces, learned judges, simulators, and formal checks can establish in their place, and what safety requires beyond task success. For learning, we discuss how tested programs are retained as experience, in skill libraries and in model weights. Finally, we turn to benchmarks and evaluation practice and outline open challenges. Writing programs is becoming cheap, while trustworthy evidence about what they do remains costly. Progress will therefore depend on a physical counterpart of continuous integration: verification cheap enough to run often, reliable enough to trust under search, and maintained alongside the experience it validates.

</details>


### [368] [FIRE-Drive: Feasibility-Informed Risk Evaluation for End-to-End Diffusion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22297)

Abstract: Diffusion-based end-to-end autonomous driving models have shown strong potential in generating multimodal planning trajectories. However, their performance still depends heavily on trajectory initialization, mode evaluation, and supervision quality. Weakly constrained noisy initialization may introduce infeasible proposals and increase the burden of denoising. Moreover, selecting the optimal mode remains challenging when candidate trajectories are geometrically similar but differ in safety, interaction risk, and progress. To address these issues, we propose FIRE-Drive, a feasibility-informed risk evaluation framework for end-to-end autonomous driving. FIRE-Drive improves diffusion-based planning through dynamic feasible anchor initialization, candidate-specific bird's-eye-view risk evidence, and compact high-quality candidate supervision with lightweight refinement. This design aligns the initial support, risk-aware scoring, and multimodal supervision of trajectory modes under a unified planning objective. It enables the diffusion decoder to focus on refining plausible trajectories while the scorer distinguishes modes using local risk evidence. Experiments on NAVSIM v1 show that FIRE-Drive achieves a Predictive Driver Model Score of 91.5 with a ResNet34 backbone. Additional closed-loop evaluation on 220 Bench2Drive routes yields a Driving Score of 71.43 and a Success Rate of 42.73%. Component ablations and a scorer comparison under matched candidate inputs support the contributions of feasible initialization, auxiliary supervision, and risk-informed selection. Our code is available at https://github.com/Latte00/FIRE-drive.

</details>

Abstract: Human data can support robot policy pretraining, but gains attributed to a data recipe may also reflect subset size, training variability, or downstream adaptation. In an HPT implementation on a 20-task MetaWorld suite with three training blocks, a constrained random 5,000-recording subset matches most of the task-informed subset's gain over the 40,000-recording pool at 20,000 updates (3.69 of 4.29 percentage points), leaving equal-size selection differences of 0.60 points at 5,000 recordings and 0.45 points at 1,250 recordings, with wide paired intervals. Across 12 comparisons, task orderings repeat across seeds for several contrasts, but a fixed predictor built from the other two seeds trails the mean or difficulty reference in at least one held-out fold for every comparison. Neither mechanism probe shows its predicted positive interaction: task-slot mismatch yields an interaction of $-0.0347$ points after adaptation, and in a six-task observation experiment the observation-by-pretraining interaction changes sign between seeds ($-18.29$ and $+1.04$ points) while full-state pretraining gains are positive in both ($+6.37$ and $+2.31$ points). This retrospective study shows how equal-size and capacity controls, seed-level reporting, and targeted interventions change the interpretation of apparent transfer gains.

</details>


### [370] [Beyond Episodic Recall: Experience-to-Knowledge Evolution for Lifelong Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22321)
*Weitao An,Chenghao Xu,Xu Yang,Cheng Deng*

Abstract: Lifelong embodied navigation requires agents to accumulate experience through continuous interaction and reuse it for subsequent goal searches. Existing memory-enhanced methods typically retain historical observations or construct structured scene representations. However, storing observations or high-dimensional semantic embeddings incurs growing storage costs. Meanwhile, structured representations support retrieval and semantic reasoning but do not explicitly evolve accumulated experience into environment-specific knowledge. To address these limitations, we propose Experience-Evolving Memory (EvoNav), a novel two-timescale memory framework. It organizes semantic observations, spatial relations, and task-feedback cues into sparse experience atoms. During online operation, experience atoms continually consolidate repeated observations and form dynamic links based on transitions. At the slower timescale, EvoNav distills accumulated category co-occurrences and feedback signals into environment-specific structural knowledge without relying on predefined object--room relations. For each new goal, EvoNav combines direct goal evidence from experience atoms with learned structural knowledge to guide goal-directed search on the current traversability map. Experiments on single-goal, sequential multi-floor, and multimodal navigation demonstrate effective experience reuse and improved path efficiency with compact persistent memory, together with successful transfer to a real robot.

</details>


### [371] [SR-VLA: Stall-Reduced VLA Execution via Predicted-Input Lookahead and Selective KV Cache Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22356)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies incur control stalls when inference blocks the next action chunk. Asynchronous early inference can hide latency, but its results may be inconsistent with the arriving actual inputs. Unconditional reuse risks acting on mismatched inputs, whereas full recomputation discards useful precomputation. We propose SR-VLA, combining predicted-input lookahead with selective KV cache repair. During current chunk execution, a lightweight action-conditioned predictor generates next-boundary visual observations and proprioceptive state. These predictions, together with the task instruction, drive the frozen VLA to precompute a speculative KV cache and action chunk. After real inputs are available to VLA, predicted--real differences and task conditions determine whether to accept the action chunk or trigger selective KV repair. On LIBERO, SR-VLA achieves $2.67\times$ and $1.27\times$ query speedups over the respective synchronous $π_{0.5}$ and OpenVLA-OFT baselines, while maintaining competitive average task success rates. Under target-motion perturbation, success remains baseline-comparable, with $1.52\times$--$1.93\times$ query speedup.

</details>

Abstract: Robot failures are difficult to collect at scale, limiting the range of failure scenarios represented in robot learning datasets. We present FailureGen, a method for generating synthetic videos with temporal event annotations by reusing action trajectories from recorded failures. The method comprises four stages: (1) fine-tuning an action-conditioned Cosmos world model on successful and failed real executions; (2) annotating source recordings to identify failure-associated donor segments and compatible successful hosts; (3) transferring donor motion into host contexts, with optional spatial offsets, to generate continuous video rollouts; and (4) annotating and filtering the generated samples according to observed events and visual quality. We study two implementations with different tasks and robot configurations, with visual failure detection serving as one downstream application. In a preliminary comparison, a detector trained on real and generated data achieves an average precision of 0.51, compared with 0.41 using real data alone, and detects 33 of 36 failure intervals, compared with 28 of 36. By reusing observed failures in additional contexts, FailureGen expands the data available for studying robot failures and developing downstream learning methods.

</details>


### [373] [DGV-DLIVO: Degeneracy-Guided Visual Fusion for Direct LiDAR-Inertial-Visual Odometry in Degenerate Tunnel Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22385)

Abstract: Reliable localization in long underground infrastructures remains a major challenge for autonomous inspection robots. In hydropower spillway tunnels and drainage galleries, repetitive geometric structures provide insufficient constraints along the tunnel axis, while weak texture and illumination variations further degrade visual estimation. Under this coupled degradation, existing LiDAR-inertial and visual-inertial odometry systems may lose the ability to recover continuous longitudinal motion. This paper presents DGV-DLIVO, a degeneracy-guided visual fusion for direct LiDAR-inertial-visual odometry for extreme tunnel environments. Instead of treating vision as a general complementary sensor, the proposed method exploits the LiDAR-degenerate direction as an active guidance signal to determine informative visual regions, establish LiDAR-anchored visual associations, and perform subspace-selective fusion. The visual constraints are introduced only into the deficient motion subspace, while unreliable LiDAR information in the same direction is adaptively suppressed and reliable geometric constraints are preserved. Real-world experiments in hydropower tunnels demonstrate that representative state-of-the-art LIO, LIVO, and VIO methods suffer from stagnation, oscillation, severe under-travel, curved drift, or divergence, whereas DGV-DLIVO maintains continuous and directionally consistent tunnel-axis motion estimation under both cart-mounted and handheld acquisition. It achieves a 91.4% progress ratio over the 40m tunnel sequence and significantly improves longitudinal recovery and trajectory stability. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of degeneracy-guided adaptive fusion for robotic inspection in severely degraded underground environments. The source code and a supplementary video are publicly available at https://github.com/0RayZhang0/DGV-DLIVO.

</details>


### [374] [Hidden Variables Behind Real-Robot Data Quality: Lessons from Hundreds of Thousands of Hours](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22388)
*Maniformer Real-World Manipulation Team,Che Tsang Calvin,Xingyu Chen,Bowen Jiang,Naixiang Gao,Xiongfeng Cai,Enlin Gu,Ruiying Li,Peize Li,Mianrui Zhu,Haomiao Zhang,Xin He,Minhui Yu,Jun Zhao,Zihao Zhu,Yang Chen,Jingmei Jin,Keru Zhou,Chen Wang,Chang Zhou,Zhiwen Hou,Di Yang,Wenjun Sun,Fan Yang,Liyan Zhang,Yi Lu,Haojun Ma,Jun Zhu,Pengxin Zha,Zhaosheng Wang,Xin Zhao,Xindong He,Chiming Liu,Jianheng Song,Jiayao Ma,Maoqing Yao,Guanghui Ren,Qian Wang,Wei Shan,Yang Yang,Li Liu,Wenhao Wang*

Abstract: As real-robot data collection scales, collection-system design shapes both operator efficiency and the behaviors learned by robot policies. Workspace limitations, control-interface friction, and latency can introduce pauses, detours, and corrective motions into otherwise successful demonstrations. We investigate these effects across embodiment, teleoperation control, and data consistency, combining a kinematic study of 25 dual-arm robots, controlled teleoperation experiments, and real-robot evaluation of fine-tuned $π_{0.5}$ policies. The embodiment study shows that positional reachability overstates orientation-aware task coverage and that modest shoulder motion can substantially expand the effective workspace. Teleoperation experiments show that parallelizing body-part control reduces task completion time by 43-53%, while compliant control improves success and reduces peak contact forces on constrained and contact-rich tasks. Policy-learning experiments show that demonstrations must capture the visual information underlying action choices: active-search demonstrations improve arm-selection accuracy from chance (50%) to 100%. Calibration experiments reveal that mixed-calibration training data remain usable in our tested configurations when an inference-aligned subset is retained, whereas inference-time miscalibration causes all 30 trials to fail. Policies also retain approximately 90% of the extra path length in detour-contaminated demonstrations, showing how inefficient operator motions persist in learned behavior. Together, these findings connect collection-system design to demonstration quality and downstream policy execution. They motivate evaluating effective workspace, control efficiency, observation completeness, calibration alignment, and motion quality before scaling collection, and addressing system-induced compensations at their source.

</details>


### [375] [SkiNet-Sim: Simulating Petri Nets with Data for the Verification and Validation of Controllers for Autonomous Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22397)

Abstract: A large amount of high-level Petri net formalisms has been investigated and deployed in academic settings over the past decades, but these deployments fail to reach practical applications, despite the strong guarantees they can bring to stakeholders. In particular, the lack of direct benefits from end-users reduces the appeal of Petri nets, both due to the overly simple classical Petri net formalism, and the overly specialized high-level formalisms, on top of state-space explosion issues.
  In this work, we investigate how a middle-ground could be reached, by separating the implementation-specific aspects of software or hardware behaviors, from the classical Petri net semantics. The goal is to benefit from the sought-after aspects of Petri nets, namely their simple firing semantic and model-checking capabilities, while still being able to augment their behavior to best reflect the modelled system. We present a formal model of Interpreted Petri nets with data-aware transitions and tokens. We illustrate this formalism by modelling the executive layer of a robotic system, and provide a lightweight tool to augment classical Petri nets for general purpose applications, as well as data-aware state-space exploration for model-checking. Their specialization in the context of autonomous robotics is showcased, with a complete industrial inspection mission use-case, to compare simulation and model-checking results between the classical and the new data-aware Petri net semantic.

</details>

Abstract: Learning-based methods now let humanoids run robustly, and they have begun to compete in athletic events. Yet few reports describe a complete autonomous humanoid running system in an actual race, where the robot must follow a lane at a new venue, pace itself within hardware limits, and finish without intervention. We report such a system deployed on the Unitree G1, a commercially available general-purpose humanoid, in the 100 m, 400 m, and 1500 m events of the World Humanoid Robot Games 2026. The system integrates vision-based lane perception, path tracking, and a running policy trained by reinforcement learning with a human motion prior. To address the challenges of humanoid running, we stabilize head-camera images against body motion and use IMU-assisted leg odometry to bridge flight phases. To manage motor heating, we penalize hip-pitch torque during training, add heat-dissipation plates, and apply temperature-based speed limiting with event-specific pacing. The robot placed 13th of 74 in the 100 m, 10th of 44 in the 400 m first round before falling in the semifinal, and seventh of 35 in the 1500 m. In this deployment, thermal margin set the pace in every event, and the semifinal fall followed a lane-detection error.

</details>

Abstract: Current B-Spline trajectory planners face a critical trade-off: they either sacrifice computational speed to maintain strict Safe Flight Corridor (SFC) guarantees, or they abandon hard safety bounds in favor of soft-constraint penalties to achieve real-time performance. We present COAST (Corridor-Optimized Aerially-Safe Trajectories), a B-Spline based, online trajectory planner capable of formulating snap minimized trajectories with SFC guarantees. COAST divides the problem into two stages: an A* based front-end that generates geometrically simple SFCs (represented as a chain of rectangular prisms), and a quadratic programming back-end for control point optimization. To handle the non-convexity of SFC intersections, we leverage the finite support property of B-Splines, to ensure the spline never exits SFC boundaries. The control point optimization incorporates MINVO transforms for kinodynamic bounding, allowing COAST to design non-conservative, dynamically feasible trajectories, while a snap minimization matrix calculation provides flight smoothness. This formulation provides a formal geometric containment guarantee, while ensuring mean trajectory computation times under 5 ms for real-time deployment. We validate our results through high-fidelity simulation experiments. The source code can be found at: https://github.com/byu-magicc/Minimum-Snap-Trajectories

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models integrate visual perception, language understanding, and action generation to control robots. Testing these systems produces trajectories and task outcomes, but existing approaches provide limited guidance on using these recorded trajectories to assess subsequent executions or inform policy repair. We conjecture that, for a given policy and task, traversing certain workspace regions is associated with a higher likelihood of failure. To investigate this conjecture, we propose VoxelSBFL, a black-box adaptation of Spectrum-Based Fault Localization (SBFL) in which workspace voxels replace program elements as units of analysis. Recorded tool-center-point (TCP) trajectories provide binary voxel coverage, which is combined with execution outcomes to construct suspiciousness maps using established SBFL formulas. These maps identify failure-associated regions and score unseen trajectories without requiring internal model access. We evaluate 34 spectrum-based techniques across four VLA models using 96,000 Pick-Up trajectories in simulation and compare the leading techniques against eight action- and trajectory-based metrics. VoxelSBFL obtains an overall macro ROC-AUC of 0.704, significantly outperforming state-of-the art baselines. Sensitivity analyses show that performance varies with spatial granularity and training-set size. These findings support spatial spectra for VLA execution assessment and motivate future research on runtime monitoring, self-healing and targeted policy improvement.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents the design and implementation of Auriga Robotics' mobile manipulator robot, developed at the Robotics and Intelligent Automation Laboratory, Department of Electrical Engineering, Shahid Beheshti University. Hereafter, this platform is referred to as the @Work Robot. The robot targets industrial tasks, with an emphasis on improving efficiency in repetitive or hazardous work environments. It uses a 4-wheel Mecanum drive for omnidirectional movement and a 5-degree-of-freedom manipulator arm with a 3D-printed gripper for object handling and navigation. The electronics consist of custom PCB boards built around ESP32 microcontrollers, together with an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX that handles high-level processing and real-time system control. On the software side, Hector SLAM provides mapping and localization, A* handles path planning, and YOLO performs real-time object detection. These modules draw on fused sensor data to deliver robust navigation and reliable collision avoidance. Overall, the system shows effective hardware-software integration, with consistent performance in autonomous navigation, object handling, and task execution in industrial-like settings.

Abstract: A task specifies how the world should change, while a policy specifies what the robot should do. We introduce Progress Predicts Policy (PPP), which learns a language-conditioned scalar field through the commands induced by its differential. We obtain action conditioning by composing a latent progress field with a shared world model and read its action gradient at a fixed reference command. Continuous commands scale this gradient by a learned positive gain, while discrete commands use finite differences of the same scalar. This yields a hybrid policy without a separate action head or iterative search. Fitting demonstration progress values alone does not ensure that action gradients align with expert commands. PPP trains the induced commands by imitation and uses double backpropagation for the continuous gradient readout to shape the field's local control geometry. Demonstration timing calibrates field values on observed states without additional annotation. The continuous readout remains learnable without progress labels but these timing-derived targets support progress monitoring during execution. Experiments in simulated and real robot manipulation show that PPP outperforms behavioral cloning under a shared architecture.

</details>

Abstract: Failure avoidance in complex robot-environment interactions is challenging because the underlying dynamics are difficult to model accurately, and available predictive models may not provide reliable derivatives. We present Sim2CBF, a failure-avoidance framework that solves the Control Barrier Function (CBF)-Safety Filter (SF) optimization problem using only forward-model evaluations, thereby avoiding differentiation of the predictive model. Specifically, we approximate the CBF constraint using finite differences and local linear interpolation over sampled control inputs while preserving failure-avoidance guarantees. The underlying CBF is implicit and constructed from backup-policy rollouts, enabling the use of expressive predictive models such as physics-based simulators and learned world models. Compared with purely sampling-based SFs, our formulation requires fewer control samples and yields less conservative behavior than direct nominal-backup blending. We evaluate Sim2CBF in simulation, hardware box-toppling, and vision-based control tasks, demonstrating its applicability to contact-rich and learned dynamics, as well as its adaptability to changes in the predictive model.

</details>

Abstract: Nonlinear filtering with state-dependent multiplicative measurement noise poses a notable challenge for spacecraft attitude determination and robotic pose estimation. This work develops the FPF-DM, a decomposition scheme for feedback particle filters tailored to this family of filtering problems. Extending prior decomposition techniques for additive noise and polynomial measurement models, the proposed method resolves the key difficulty that state-dependent multiplicative noise breaks the polynomial assumption and prevents Hermite spectral backward recursion for the weighted Poisson's equation. By measure transformation and importance reweighting, we incorporate the inverse measurement covariance into particle weights and rescale the feedback gain to restore the polynomial-form Poisson's equation, with the decomposition generalized to non-uniformly weighted mixtures for closed-form gain computation. This general FPF-DM is instantiated on quaternion and dual-quaternion manifolds for attitude tracking and point-cloud registration. Simulations on 4D attitude tracking and 8D point-cloud registration verify that the instantiated filters using only 10 particles achieve superior accuracy and computational performance relative to baseline filters.

</details>

Abstract: Collective estimation under unreliable sensing requires robots to adaptively balance personal observations and social information from peers. We propose DGF-Asym, a decentralized collective estimation method extending the DeGroot-Friedkin model with asymmetric peer weighting based on retrospective predictive performance. The method supports consensus formation without prior sensor-noise statistics or explicit fault models while keeping inter-robot communication asynchronous. We evaluate DGF-Asym in simulated collective navigation under noisy observations and time-varying sensor faults. Compared with three baselines, it produces more stable estimates and more reliably sustains the decision criterion, at the cost of longer decision times. However, overall performance depends on fault ratio, group size, and influence-threshold selection. Our results demonstrate the potential of adaptive peer weighting for stable model-free collective estimation under unreliable sensing.

</details>

Abstract: Magnetic levitation with six-degrees-of-freedom enables frictionless transport and micrometer-scale positioning in principle, but low-level control is inherently challenging due to its complex and unstable dynamics. Industrial systems typically rely on deep hardware-software co-design, where mover and stator geometries are jointly designed to enable a compact, hand-crafted approximation of the electromagnetic force computation, thereby keeping control computationally tractable at kilohertz rates. However, this approach imposes overly conservative constraints on the operating workspace and the range of supported mover topologies. Instead of reducing the underlying physics to a hand-crafted approximation, we compute the physics directly. We introduce Magflow, a fully differentiable, GPU-parallel simulator for 6D magnetic levitation. It is used to train the first neural controller for an industrial-grade 6D magnetic levitation system that is learned purely from simulated physics. We show that the learned neural controller transfers zero-shot from simulation to real hardware while satisfying hard real-time constraints at 4 kHz. The learned controller achieves qualitatively new behaviors and even levitates a mover topology for which no hand-crafted solution exists. These results demonstrate that co-design is not a physical necessity for 6D magnetic levitation control. More broadly, control learning from simulated physics can find solutions beyond the reach of control engineering in demanding industrial domains. Source code and demonstration videos are publicly available at https://sites.google.com/view/magflow.

</details>

Abstract: Visual world models predict future visual states conditioned on actions, but recursive errors in entity poses and spatial relations limit their long-horizon prediction for robot planning and control. We ask whether a separately predicted relational state can correct this drift without restructuring the visual model. This is challenging because graph nodes and visual states share neither elements nor a feature space. We propose Joint Graph--Visual Dynamics Correction (JointGV), a plug-in based on a cross-granularity dynamics hypothesis: despite their different representations, graph-node and visual-state changes describe the same physical transition. JointGV rolls out a task-conditioned scene graph alongside the visual world model, couples their predicted changes, and combines the resulting graph-motion context with graph-predicted node positions to compute a bounded residual. Graph-learning and correction gradients do not propagate into the original visual dynamics. We attach JointGV to DINO-WM (spatial tokens) and DreamerV3 (a global latent state), evaluating both on DMC Cheetah Run and Walker Walk across three seeds and extending the evaluation to contact-rich manipulation with DMC Finger Turn Hard. Across 32-step rollouts, JointGV improves structural and visual-prediction accuracy, reducing joint-position error by 11--28\%. For spatial tokens, learned graph--visual change coupling gives lower error than broadcasting the same mean graph-motion context to every token, particularly when non-adjacent parts appear close together in the image. On real-robot inverted-pendulum sequences, JointGV reduces DINO-WM's 32-step pole-angle error from $12.56^\circ$ to $6.69^\circ$ and improves DreamerV3 and LeWorldModel as well. In simulated inverted-pendulum control, an actor trained with JointGV-corrected rollouts completes 46 of 50 episodes, compared with none using the original rollouts.

</details>

Abstract: Existing agentic manipulation systems treat pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policies as opaque, language-only primitives: when a policy call fails under unfamiliar instructions or scene perturbations, the agent cannot steer its behavior and is forced into futile retries or costly manual takeover. We present InteractVLA, a framework that turns pretrained flow-based VLAs into geometrically steerable and inspectable tools through a bidirectional geometric interface with vision-language agents. In the forward direction, test-time geometric guidance translates an agent-specified end-effector target into input gradients through the action expert via a differentiable command-to-pose mapping, while soft-time target alignment treats arrival time as a latent execution index to match the target pose without distorting the policy's approach motion. In the reverse direction, action-informed agentic refinement exposes generated action chunks and calibrated camera reprojections back to the agent, allowing it to diagnose spatial discrepancies, revise targets for subsequent VLA calls, or locally adjust motion segments on top of the guided trajectory. Making the VLA controllable not only boosts online agentic tool use, but also unlocks effective recursive self-improvement (RSI), achieving state-of-the-art performance of 99.4\% on LIBERO-Plus, 48.62\% on RoboDojo, and 89.4\% on RoboCasa365. Project page: https://lingwupro.github.io/InteractVLA/.

</details>

Abstract: The agents in a heterogeneous team can cooperate to accomplish tasks that exceed their individual capabilities. The team trajectories for a cooperative task can be generated by a generative model, provided that the model has been trained on demonstrations of the agents cooperating. Yet, demonstrations of cooperation are costly and may not exist for tasks that the team has never performed. The conditions under which the agents can combine their capabilities, however, are physical properties of the team and are known before any demonstration is collected. We encode the conditions as constraints on the team trajectories, and we enforce the constraints while sampling from generative models trained on demonstrations of each agent alone. We prove that the proposed method can generate every team trajectory that satisfies the constraints, although the demonstrations contain no cooperation. Hence the constraints determine which team trajectories can be generated, and the demonstrations determine how likely each team trajectory is. Numerical results on two cooperative tasks show the effectiveness of the method, which generates cooperative team trajectories that satisfy the constraints and requires no retraining when agents join or leave the team. Finally, we implement the method on a multi-robot testbed.

</details>

Abstract: Data-driven predictive control (DDPC) predicts system responses from recorded trajectories, but nonlinear dynamics can limit the accuracy of fixed linear predictors. This paper studies geometric robust DDPC for trajectory tracking of a fully actuated manipulator using model-based feedback linearisation. A mechanical model maps virtual acceleration commands into joint torques, while recorded data capture residual dynamics arising from imperfect model parameters. The controller minimises worst-case prediction error over an open Grassmannian ball centred at the fitted trajectory subspace. Simulations on a planar 2R manipulator under gravity compare the proposed method with double-integrator model predictive control (MPC) and direct regularised data-enabled predictive control (DeePC). Results show improved tracking over double-integrator MPC despite increased torque effort, while performance relative to DeePC depends on the reference trajectory.

</details>

Abstract: Successful manipulation requires the manipulated objects to progress toward a desired task outcome. Generative policies can produce plausible robot motions without ensuring this progress, allowing successive action chunks to stall or undo earlier changes to the scene. We introduce a task-centric inference-time refinement framework for flow matching policies that evaluates candidate action chunks through parallel physics-based rollouts and searches for motions satisfying sufficient conditions for task convergence. Task-level performance funnels bound temporary regressions while requiring net progress over each chunk. We establish sufficient conditions under which repeated funnel satisfaction yields convergence throughout execution, and use interval-wise Lipschitz bounds to verify continuous-time funnel satisfaction from sampled rollouts. To accommodate contact-rich hybrid dynamics, we refine the flow's source seeds using derivative-free Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (CMA-ES), constrained to radial quantile bands of the source distribution to help retain the learned motion prior. This formulation supports both Euclidean and pose-valued Riemannian Flow Matching (RMFM) policies. On PushT and three-cube stacking, refinement raises success from 31.3% to 88.5% and from 77.0% to 86.0%. On PushT, whose coverage score responds continuously to the motion of the block, a learned funnel lowers the average maximum loss of achieved progress by 19% relative to constraining the endpoint of each chunk alone, at a similar success rate. With the radial band, 11.3% of the executed motion windows are out of distribution, a rate within the range of held-out demonstrations, compared with 34.1% for an unconstrained search.

</details>

Abstract: Generalised referring expression comprehension (GREC) aims to identify and localise the zero, one or multiple objects referred to by a language expression. In robotic manipulation, grounding also needs to remain accurate when requests for the same objects are paraphrased or embedded in additional context in robotics. However, grounding expert models are sensitive to instruction variation, while generative vision--language models (VLMs) struggle with complete target recovery and rejection of absent targets. These complementary limitations motivate combining the contextual representations of VLMs with the direct spatial prediction capabilities of grounding experts. We therefore introduce VLM-guided Grounding Expert (VGE), which combines a frozen VLM with a trainable grounding expert through layerwise joint attention and text-conditioned instance scoring. These interfaces leverage multimodal understanding of the VLM to guide object-query refinement and candidate selection while retaining direct bounding-box prediction. Across three datasets, VGE achieves the highest averaged F1 score among the evaluated methods on all five test splits, with differences of at most 1.2 percentage points across instruction conditions within each split. On the dataset with robotic scenes, VGE also achieves the highest F1 across instruction variants and no-target, single-target and multiple-target cases. VGE provides a $5.3$--$13.0\times$ inference speedup over the baseline with the same VLM base model. These results support combining VLM context with specialised spatial prediction to address both grounding challenges, with particularly strong performance in robotic scenes. Code will be released upon publication.

</details>


### [391] [Risk Density RRT$^*$: Planning in the Space of Probability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22654)

Abstract: Planning under uncertainty is a fundamental problem in robotics. While much of the literature seeks minimum-cost paths subject to probabilistic constraints, this paper instead considers collision probability, or risk classically defined as probability times severity, directly as the planning objective. We propose Risk Density RRT$^*$ (RD-RRT$^*$), an extension of RRT$^*$ for high-level planning in environments with deterministic and uncertain obstacles. RD-RRT$^*$ builds on Risk Density, a functional that evaluates a continuous path as a whole and provides a tractable approximation of its collision probability. The framework also supports severity-weighted risk and a hybrid variant that adds a path-length penalty, balancing risk and geometric efficiency while regularizing exploration in low-density regions. We validate the method across a wide range of scenarios. The results show that RD-RRT$^*$ consistently finds paths with lower estimated collision probability by optimizing a global risk-based objective. We further demonstrate the fidelity of the underlying approximation through Monte Carlo simulations. Finally, we discuss how the topological structure induced by Risk Density may provide a useful representation of the environment and offer insights into risk-aware planning.

</details>

Abstract: World models (WMs) generate driving scenarios beyond recorded logs, enabling the training and evaluation of autonomous driving systems in unseen interactions. However, existing WMs struggle to ensure both behavioral realism and visual fidelity, since they overlook the relationship between vehicle interactions and the ego visual observations. We propose a closed-loop interactive WM that couples behavior modeling and visual rendering. First, imitation-learned background-vehicle (BV) policies are fine-tuned with multi-agent reinforcement learning in the closed loop against an end-to-end ego planner that reacts to the rendered images, so that their interaction behaviors match those in real logs. Second, the viewpoints exposed by these interactions guide a per-Gaussian diagnosis of each 3D Gaussian vehicle asset, which attenuates view-dependent colors on under-observed Gaussians and fills never-observed surfaces with generated Gaussians, while preserving the observed appearance. Third, signal lights are rendered according to the driving intentions of the vehicles, by an editor that learns the light states unobserved for a vehicle through counterfactual fine-tuning with a light-state classifier. On long-tail closed-loop scenarios, our method reduces the divergence from real-world interaction behaviors by 85% and improves novel-view vehicle PSNR by 2.0dB over the state-of-the-art WM. The significance of BV light signaling in WMs is shown, as the rendered signal lights conveying BV driving intentions indirectly impact ego driving behaviors in a quarter of the scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate end-effector trajectory tracking on quadruped manipulators is challenging because uncertainties in actuators, foot-ground contact, and geometric errors are propagated through the leg-arm kinematic chain. Detecting such errors requires sensing beyond joint encoders that can capture the effects of these uncertainties on the robot's motion, since joint encoders only provide measurements at the actuated joints and cannot fully observe mechanical errors such as backlash, dimensional inaccuracies, or structural deformation. This work addresses these challenges using a reinforcement learning framework with end-effector (EE) inertial feedback, targeting local high-accuracy manipulation while maintaining a nominal body pose and support configuration without intentional body motion or foot repositioning. The learned policy uses EE inertial measurements to compensate for tracking errors induced by uncertainties throughout the leg-arm system that cannot be fully inferred from proprioception alone. Simulation and real-world experiments on continuous EE trajectory tracking show overall improvements relative to nominal and proprioception-only baselines. In real-world experiments, the proposed policy reduces the MoCap-based RMSE by 17.3% and 28.0% relative to the nominal baseline under the two in-distribution (ID) command conditions, respectively, and by an additional 12.6% and 15.9% relative to the proprioception-only baseline. Furthermore, the trajectory-wise RMSE between FK-reconstructed and motion-capture-measured end-effector positions averaged 12.61+-2.15mm (mean+-standard deviation) across 24 controller-trajectory combinations, revealing a substantial discrepancy between nominal kinematic reconstruction and measured end-effector motion.

</details>

Abstract: Currently, robots can learn whole-body loco-manipulation through scale: the strongest policies have billions of parameters and still need task-specific fine-tuning. Humans have limited processing capacity, yet learn and act efficiently through a body schema that organizes what they see and how they move. We introduce Body Schema Policy (BSP), an imitation learning policy that builds such a schema into the vision-action interface. Its latent body schema places joint and link tokens on the robot's kinematic tree, where learned screw motions carry features between links. Through body-queried vision, each body part retrieves its own visual evidence, which conditions the denoising of its actions. With this inductive bias, BSP returns an action chunk faster than any policy we compare. Compact, body-specific visual readouts also emerge without spatial supervision: manipulation commands depend strongly on task objects, while locomotion commands respond more broadly to the scene. On nine simulated Unitree G1 whole-body tasks, BSP matches the $Ψ_0$ humanoid foundation model with $75\times$ fewer parameters and nearly doubles Diffusion Policy's success. The same holds on a real G1 and a Fetch mobile manipulator.

</details>

However, the role of stochasticity in the diffusion model-sampler across denoising remains poorly understood. We introduce a per-timestep counterfactual diagnostic that tests how changing the sampled noise affects the final output. Across five model-sampler configurations spanning three robotics benchmarks, diverse architectures, domains, and conditioning mechanisms, we reveal highly varied temporal sensitivity profiles, where noise-induced deviations can amplify, partially contract, or undergo near-total attenuation downstream. From these profiles, we define stochastic diversity windows (SDWs): compact intervals that identify where sampler stochasticity has the strongest measured influence on the final output. Guided by these windows, we apply two heuristic interventions: targeted stochastic amplification, which increases sample diversity exploration of the solution space, and an SDW-hybrid sampler, which replaces DDPM steps with DDIM skips in low-impact regions, resulting in network evaluation reductions of up to 40-60% and wall-clock time by up to 85%, with no consistent changes detected in feasibility, cost, or diversity across held-out tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Passive back exoskeletons show promising benefits for reducing occupational musculoskeletal disorders. However, their assistance cannot be modulated towards the task requirements. Active exoskeletons offer opportunities for pose-specific assistance, but, developing these systems from scratch for research can be time consuming. Therefore, here, we present an accelerated design pipeline that retrofits a Commercial Off-The-Shelf (COTS) passive back exoskeleton, Laevo Flex, with COTS actuation (CubeMars AK10-9) and sensing (IMUs and load-cell) paradigms. Levio was able to provide assistance based on the user's pose. Nine participants performed stoop lifting with the original Laevo and the retrofit Levio system, in transparent and assisted modes. Compared to Laevo, Levio modulated the assistance provided between the bending and lifting phases of the stoop task. The passive assistance offered by Laevo was $25.2 \pm 4.3$ Nm and $30.6 \pm 9.5$ Nm respectively during the bending and lifting phases of the stoop tasks. Levio reduced the resistive torque during bending to $17.9 \pm 4.6$ Nm and offered increased assistance of $37.7 \pm 6.4$ Nm during lifting. Moreover, participants reported higher usability scores and lowest mean global discomfort score while using the Levio when compared to Laevo. These benefits demonstrates retrofitting passive exoskeletons is a viable direction for rapid prototyping of wearable exoskeletons for lifting.

</details>

Abstract: Robot demonstrations are collected on different embodiments, leaving many robot-skill combinations unobserved. Pooling these data shares visual knowledge, while joint labels remain tied to the demonstrating kinematic chain. We propose a geometry-supervised training framework for zero-shot composition of familiar robots and skills. The central insight is that a demonstrated command provides a tool-space target for every robot that can realize it. Calibrated forward kinematics supplies this supervision to foreign native action interfaces without their joint labels for that skill. Native reconstruction retains demonstrated configurations, and positive reachability checks select targets for cross-robot supervision. A visual policy learns to predict shared actions and executes through the target robot's native decoder. During policy learning, one prediction receives geometric supervision through both source and foreign native realizations. Matched supervision controls, repeated policy training and evaluations across three backbones and two simulators demonstrate improved transfer to missing robot-task combinations.

</details>

Abstract: Conventional balance formulations often assume a rigid, stationary support, decoupling body stabilization from support motion. This assumption becomes inadequate on mechanically responsive supports, creating a coupled control challenge: corrective body motions intended to restore equilibrium can also excite support motion. We formulate sustained balance as a coupled regulation problem that coordinates equilibrium restoration with suppression of motion induced by the robot's own corrections. We introduce LISST (Lyapunov-Inspired Sustained Stabilization), a learning framework that accounts for how earlier actions shape the conditions for subsequent corrections. It uses recent observations to support immediate adjustments and longer histories to characterize persistent motion, with physical supervision relating the learned representation to robot-support behavior. To evaluate corrective actions, LISST uses coupled dynamics to assess both imbalance and the direction of motion. A Lyapunov-inspired criterion measures progress over finite windows, accommodating the transients required to restore balance. This physical evaluation guides joint-level policy learning through backpropagation through differentiable model rollouts. Privileged guidance focuses training on difficult states reached by the deployment policy and is transferred through distillation. The resulting policy operates using only onboard proprioception. Real-world experiments demonstrate that LISST maintains balance while limiting residual oscillations during continuous interaction with a dynamically responsive support. Video: youtu.be/iGHU7S4wPyw.

</details>

Abstract: Lidar-inertial odometry (LIO) has an efficiency problem. High-resolution lidars produce millions of points per second that must be processed online on the resource-constrained hardware of robots, cars, or drones, yet most LIO methods neither bound their memory nor report their compute footprint. We introduce Pelorus, a resource-aware LIO with a fixed-capacity multiresolution map of voxelized normal distributions and an iterated equivariant state estimator. A relevance-based eviction keeps the map memory bounded, and a maturity-weighted map update suppresses inconsistent observations. The pipeline runs in parallel on CPUs and GPUs. On five sequences from three public datasets, Pelorus achieves the lowest relative pose error in seven of ten metrics with a small, bounded memory footprint and, with GPU offloading, the lowest CPU load of all compared methods. On an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX, it processes a 128-beam lidar at 10 Hz. Pelorus is open source with CUDA, Vulkan, and Metal backends.

</details>

Abstract: Natural language-guided UAV navigation enables intuitive human-robot interaction and flexible autonomous flight in complex environments. However, reliable navigation requires accurate instruction following, continuous task progress tracking, and safe obstacle avoidance, which remain challenging for existing world-action models that primarily focus on action prediction. To address these limitations, we propose a world-action learning framework that integrates three complementary mechanisms: a language-conditioned action interface with targeted post-training for instruction-responsive motion, recurrent instruction-consumption prediction to track task progress and determine termination, and Gaussian-scene post-training to enhance obstacle avoidance through trajectory imitation and differentiable geometric feedback. Closed-loop evaluations and ablation studies demonstrate improvements in sequential task completion, safe goal reaching, and timely termination. Furthermore, direct one-step denoising reduces inference overhead, and real-world indoor and outdoor flights validate the framework's effectiveness.

</details>

Abstract: This work presents Arboreus, a realistic Boreal forest simulator, developed to support statistical validation of robotic autonomy algorithms in Boreal forest environments. Arboreus presents an automated methodology for synthesizing large-scale, data-informed, plausible forest worlds from parametric trees, height-variant terrain, and satellite-informed tree density distributions. The parametric generation of dominant Boreal tree species such as Birch, Spruce, and Pine is guided by trunk, canopy, and foliage parameters, extracted from growth studies in the Nordic areas. Tree distribution in an area of interest is guided by the Copernicus satellite dataset, which provides tree cover density and dominant species type at up to 10m raster resolution. The forest floor generation is guided by topographic data from the Swedish cadastral agency to produce realistic data-informed terrains. Given coordinates and extents of forested areas of interest, Arboreus synthesizes data-informed plausible forests, while offering flexibility to inject user observations of real forests (detail, age, dimensionality and tree count), which is critical for realistic synthetic data generation and for the testing of feature-sensitive autonomy algorithms. The applicability of Arboreus for benchmarking and validation of robot autonomy for different robot mobilities is demonstrated in Gazebosim, Unreal Engine, and Isaac Sim physics engines. The applicability of Arboreus as a source of synthetic forest data and its potential to support downstream forest resource mapping research (volume estimation, tree instance segmentation and stem attribute estimation) is also demonstrated. Arboreus has been open-sourced along with a dataset comprising 25 Boreal forest worlds of size 1000m2 corresponding to three real forest sites.

</details>

Abstract: Drifting, the deliberate control of a vehicle at large sideslip angles, is relevant to both motorsport and recovery from unexpected oversteer. Existing autonomous drifting methods typically determine drift intensity through predefined reference trajectories, optimization objectives, or learned policy rewards, rather than accepting a desired sideslip angle as an explicit input. We propose, to the best of our knowledge, the first generative drift trajectory planner explicitly conditioned on a requested peak sideslip angle. A conditional flow matching model is trained on demonstrated drift laps with hindsight-derived sideslip labels and refined through reinforcement fine-tuning. A control barrier function (CBF) correction is then applied to satisfy model-based road, tire friction, and actuator constraints at the planning waypoints. In closed-loop experiments in the racing simulator Assetto Corsa, a nonlinear model predictive controller tracks the generated plans for six requested sideslip angles from 20 deg to 45 deg, with 30 laps (approximately 75 km) per request. The median peak sideslip angle measured across drift-corner passes follows the requested angle, with a mean absolute error of 1.2 deg across the six conditions. Ablation experiments show that reinforcement fine-tuning improves conditioning accuracy, while CBF correction substantially reduces off-road and spin events.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate shape estimation of Deformable Linear Objects (DLOs) is a fundamental challenge in robotic manipulation, often hindered by visual occlusions and the high-dimensional configuration space of flexible materials. While Force/Torque (F/T) and vision-centric methods have been proposed, they frequently struggle with grasp-site ambiguities and environmental interference. In this letter, we investigate the hypothesis that global shape estimation can be achieved through tactile sensing and proprioception, without any visual perception. We contend that dense, high-resolution tactile imprints capture critical boundary conditions and contact gradients that are sufficient to resolve the high-dimensional configuration of the DLO. To prove this, we introduce a Physics-Informed Vision Transformer (ViT) framework that maps tactile images to a 3D vertex chain. By leveraging self-attention mechanisms and a physics-weighted loss function, our model enforces structural priors to ensure that the predicted geometry adheres to the object's physical manifold. Experimental results demonstrate that our tactile-based approach achieves superior reconstruction accuracy across Euclidean and Chamfer metrics. Furthermore, we demonstrate the efficiency of this modality, achieving model transfer to novel DLO materials and geometries. Our findings suggest that tactile feedback provides a robust and computationally efficient alternative for DLO state estimation in visually degraded or occluded environments.

</details>

Abstract: Recent world action models (WAMs) drop future prediction at inference, assuming that representations learned through auxiliary future supervision still benefit action generation. However, our layerwise analysis of Fast-WAM reveals a depth-dependent mismatch: while probes decode future state transitions more accurately from deeper visual layers, the gradients and visual focuses of the future and action objectives increasingly diverge with depth, leaving much of this predictive information unused by the action pathway. To address this issue, we introduce RelayWAM, which adds an explicit transition space between the video and action backbones. Conditioned on the task instruction, robot state, and current action estimate, recurrent transition layers extract step-relevant predictive cues across depth and relay them directly into the action attention, without generating future frames at inference. Experiments show that RelayWAM substantially amplifies the benefit of future supervision, translating into stronger generalization under distribution shifts in both simulation and real-world robot tasks. On LIBERO-Plus, RelayWAM achieves 72.03% mean success, compared to 56.42% without future supervision and 51.50% for Fast-WAM.

</details>

Abstract: Large-scale robotic simulation requires diverse deformable objects with executable physical properties and realistic interaction behaviors. However, existing 3D asset collections primarily emphasize visual diversity, while scalable physical construction and verification remain challenging. We introduce RFW-S, a large-scale dataset of 83,365 simulation-verified assets spanning 485 everyday categories across five structural families: soft solids, cloth sheets, shells, garments, and bags. We develop an automated pipeline integrating reference-guided 3D generation, structure-aware physical modeling, and LLM-assisted parameter estimation and calibration. To ensure physical reliability, we introduce an independent simulation verification framework that evaluates object behaviors under interactions distinct from those used for parameter calibration. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of independent verification and characterize deformable responses under different physical configurations. We further evaluate robotic grasping in terms of both task success and object integrity, and demonstrate the physical realism of our simulated objects through sim-to-real replay of fragile-object manipulation. RFW-S provides a scalable foundation for physically grounded simulation and embodied robotic learning.

</details>

Abstract: Household robots often need to operate appliances, and smart appliances offer both control panels and device APIs. Existing embodied AI simulation environments, however, model appliances solely as physical objects. We introduce PAIR, a smart-home simulation environment grounded in the Matter protocol~\footnote{https://csa-iot.org/all-solutions/matter/}, the industry standard for smart-home devices. PAIR provides 84 smart-device assets whose functional control panels and APIs act on a shared device state, with ambient conditions that evolve in response to both. From a survey of 1,443 requests, we construct a benchmark of 40 tasks that all require object manipulation yet remain solvable without API access, with 340 human demonstrations for policy training. Whether run alone, with a high-level planner, or with an API-enabled planner, two fine-tuned VLA policies complete none of the 120 evaluation episodes. Robots rarely get far enough to use the API: even with oracle plans and API access, over 90\% of failed rollouts stall at navigating, grasping, or operating a door. With a perfect oracle in place of the robot's body, the best planner still gets through only 57.3\% of its plan. When choosing between pressing a button and calling an API, several planners go by whether they can read the panel, not by API latency.\footnote{We will release our code and data upon publication.}

</details>

Abstract: Model-based quadruped controllers usually step on a cyclic gait, which lifts the legs in a fixed order on a fixed period, wherever the safe footholds are. We present CADENCE, a learned step planner with a small action space. Every 40 ms, it picks one of at most 65 discrete choices: no step, or one leg's step to a safe foothold. It also predicts a swing time. We train CADENCE by reinforcement learning on top of a convex model predictive controller (MPC). Nothing prescribes a gait: there is no gait schedule, and the reward has no gait term, only a cost per step. The one structural constraint is that at most two legs may swing at once. The policy we analyzed developed an acyclic gait, with no fixed period or stepping order, yet it still paired diagonal legs. An inverse footstep model (IFM), fitted on a controller's logged steps, turns each foothold into a command. With only the IFM refitted, on fewer than 20,000 steps, CADENCE ran frozen on OCS2's nonlinear MPC. In simulated trials on a range of terrains (26% to 49% voids) and speeds (0.1 m/s and 0.2 m/s), it succeeded in 85.1% (mean of five seeds), against 67.8% for OCS2-Trot, 65.5% for OCS2-Single, and 39.6% in a static walk ablation of CADENCE. At 49% voids, it succeeded in 72.9%, against 43.0% for OCS2-Trot.

</details>

Abstract: A legged robot can learn to walk inside a learned simulator: a neural network that predicts the robot's next state and is run forward on its own predictions. They are usually trained and checked one step ahead, although a policy uses them for a hundred steps or more. We audit one published system end to end, without a GPU: the dynamics model of the Robotic World Model (RWM) for the ANYmal D quadruped, and the uncertainty estimate of its follow-up, RWM-U. We rebuilt the model to match the released code's outputs, losses and gradients exactly, measured 101 trained simulators, 200 compressed copies and 38 simulators of three other architectures, and trained policies inside simulators that we tested in a hand-built MuJoCo model of the robot. Training on the model's own rollouts beat one-step training at 368 steps on all ten held-out episodes (registered), although one-step training was better one step ahead. One-step checks therefore misjudge design choices: pruning 30% of the weights kept 90% of the model's skill one step ahead and 39% at 2 s. Ensemble disagreement ranked rollout error well (Spearman +0.90 at 100 steps), yet covered 4.6% of outcomes at one sigma, against 68% for a calibrated estimate, as each network's own variance collapses under the implemented loss. Two checks that need no recorded future ranked some pools of simulators well, but neither was shown better than 8-step error in a primary test. At the robot level, accuracy has not predicted walking: all 18 fine-tuned copies we tested predicted better than the released model one and eight steps ahead, yet 13 gave failing policies, and registered tests of whether any check ranks simulators by their policies were not settled. In a secondary test, running the authors' policy inside each simulator ranked them well. We also report nine of our own claims that we withdrew. Scope: one robot, one gait, simulated data, no hardware.

</details>

Abstract: While gravity is often used in mobile robotics as a global constraint for Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM) algorithms, most estimation methods rely on indirect observation of the gravity vector. In this paper, we propose the use of altimeter measurements to directly observe the direction of the gravity vector. We provide a theoretical evaluation of the estimation noise and validate it through extensive experimental data, along with an evaluation of the impact of intersensor distance on our gravity vector estimates. Experimental results demonstrate that a median error of $0.87^\circ$ on the tilt estimation can be achieved with our system in semi-static scenarios. Furthermore, we demonstrate the potential of this approach by comparing it with state-of-the-art methods based on Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs). We highlight that we achieve similar performance to that of IMU-based systems in highly dynamic scenarios and even outperform in specific cases of accelerometer measurement saturation. Finally, an additional experiment shows the potential use within a robotic system through the integration into an Iterative Closest Point (ICP) algorithm.

</details>

Abstract: Robot manipulation videos provide a rich visual experience, yet recovering robot action and gripper-state annotations from RGB observations still remains challenging. Using the embodiment-agnostic tool-center-point (TCP) pose as the action space, we present TCP4D, a unified model in which a shared 4D scene representation both tracks observed TCP motion and generates future TCP trajectories. Given a monocular video and initial 2D TCP queries, the tracker directly recovers metric TCP poses in SE(3) and gripper states alongside scene geometry and camera parameters. The generator samples local features from the same 4D representation along the recovered TCP tracks, then combines them with robot observations and language instructions to predict future TCP states. Our new cross-embodiment augmentation pipeline converts existing RGB videos into demonstrations for alternative embodiments by retargeting the recovered motion and synthesizing the corresponding observations, without recorded source-robot states. Experiments show that the tracker achieves absolute trajectory errors of 7.60 and 16.07 mm on DROID and RoboLab and adapts to a new embodiment with only ten demonstrations. The generator achieves 69.19% mean success on RoboTwin and outperforms well-established policy models on two real-world tasks, while joint training on source and augmented demonstrations improves target-robot success by 41.5% over augmented-only training.

</details>

Abstract: Scenario-based optimization provides finite-sample guarantees for decisions constructed from sampled uncertainty, but its certification procedures can be sensitive to how those samples are represented. We investigate this sensitivity under lossless scenario regrouping, which preserves the sampled information, feasible region, optimizer, and primitive violation probability. For an equal-allocation support-subsample certificate, we derive the distortion factor \(k^2(1-ε)^{k-1}\), establish certification reversals between equivalent representations, and provide sufficient conditions for acceptance dominance under nested coarsening. We further apply nondegeneracy-free Wait-and-Judge theory to coordinatewise scenario envelopes, enabling stronger certification directly from the primitive observations. In paired scenario-MPC experiments, exact regrouping increases certification availability from 318 to 471 of 480 attempts without changing the samples, numerical proposals, or requiring any reduction in witness cardinality. Primitive Wait-and-Judge certification accepts the same 471 attempts without regrouping. These results demonstrate that apparent scenario sample-efficiency gains can arise from certificate accounting rather than additional information or reduced decision risk.

</details>

Abstract: The survey compares two research traditions within the mathematical theory of designing the rules of interaction (mechanisms) for purposeful participants: mechanism design, together with contract theory and implementation theory, and the theory of active systems created by V.N. Burkov and developed into the theory of control in organizations (TCO) by D.A. Novikov's school; the main results of Yu.B. Germeier's school and of the domestic schools adjacent to active systems theory (AST) are covered as well. The problem statements and principal results of 2006-2026 are presented: for mechanism design (MD) - robust and dynamic mechanism design, multidimensional screening, information design, simplicity of mechanisms and credibility of the designer, market design, algorithmic and learning-based design, mechanisms for AI agents, and robust, relational, dynamic and algorithmic contracts; for AST and TCO - a general typology of control, strategy-proof and incentive-compatible planning mechanisms, decomposition of the agents' game, systems with distributed control and coalitional interaction, reflexive games and informational control, control of staff and structure, dynamic active systems, and project management. A correspondence between the classical results is established (the fair play principle and the revelation principle, the serial resource allocation mechanism and the uniform rule, active expertise mechanisms and generalized median schemes, quasi-compensatory incentive schemes and contracts under observable actions), and priorities are delineated by uniform rules; the areas where the two traditions diverge are identified, as are the dimensions of the problem (staff, structure, institutions, awareness, agents' beliefs) covered only by TCO.

</details>

Abstract: Managing transmission congestion in multi-area power grids dominated by inverter-based resources (IBRs) requires bridging a severe timescale and modeling divide. Supervisory congestion optimizers -- such as Online Feedback Optimization (OFO) and two-tiered differentiable Model Predictive Control (MPC) -- rely on linearized, convex algebraic grid approximations that cannot guarantee dynamic stability during sub-second converter transients. Conversely, fast distributed consensus schemes like Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM) depend on convex plant assumptions and iterative message exchanges that lag behind rapid tie-line surges. This paper replaces convex ADMM emergency consensus with a Distributed Nonlinear Separable Control Contraction Metric (SCCM) architecture spanning three nested tiers: the Device layer, the Area layer, and the Grid tie-line layer. By pairing online Prediction Error Method and Moving Horizon Estimation (PEM-MHE) parameter tracking with offline convex Linear Matrix Inequality (LMI) synthesis, the controller executes a closed-form nonlinear feedback law online without solving real-time optimization problems. We derive the Pontryagin attainable (reachable) ellipsoids for each layer, prove hierarchical tube containment via Schur-complement matrix inequalities, and project the certified Grid reachable tube through Power Transfer Distribution Factors (PTDFs) to supply guaranteed safe thermal limits to the supervisory coordinator. Validation on modified IEEE 39-bus (100% IBR, zero synchronous generators) and 3-area IEEE 118-bus systems demonstrates an 82.8% reduction in cumulative tie-line thermal overload while maintaining strict exponential contraction ($λ_{\max} \le -2.43$) even under a 3 s communication blackout.

</details>

Abstract: Virtual power plants (VPPs) trade in the same wholesale market and share distribution lines and transformers, and uncoordinated, profit-maximizing VPPs can push a shared transformer past its thermal limit or drive a feeder voltage out of band. The utility, which only observes the front-of-the-meter net injection at each bus, must keep the network safe from that net injection alone. We allocate a 24-hour active and reactive operating envelope to each VPP by a Benders decomposition: the utility sets a trial envelope, each VPP solves its private convex portfolio problem, and the returned value and dual gradient become supporting cuts that rebuild its value function while its schedule, state, and cost stay private. The envelope carries the transformer's thermal state across hours, bounds a joint conditional value-at-risk over forecast uncertainty, and is re-optimized each hour with the current hour committed. On a reduced IEEE-13 feeder with real load and prices, an active-power-only implementation lowers the transformer's peak-temperature CVaR from 254 to 97\,$^\circ$C at a 28\% value give-up and holds the transformer below its limit where nameplate access breaches it.

</details>

Abstract: The rapid growth of artificial intelligence (AI) workloads is increasing power demand in AI data centers, and processor current is projected to reach 2000 A and beyond. This trend is exposing the limitations of lateral power delivery (LPD) to processors and has motivated growing interest in vertical power delivery (VPD). This paper reviews the power delivery network (PDN) of AI data centers and the upcoming transition from LPD to VPD. First, the 800 VDC power delivery architecture, the impedance behavior of the PDN, and the limitations of LPD are discussed. Then, VPD implementation approaches, reported converter designs, and packaging technologies are examined, together with integrated voltage regulators (IVRs) and embedded passive components. In addition, power integrity simulations are presented to compare regulator and plane positions, from which a set of PDN design guidelines is derived. The results show that moving a voltage regulator module (VRM) from 50 mm away to directly below the die reduces the loop inductance by nearly half. In a complete PDN, this relocation lowers the low-frequency anti-resonant peak, whereas the mid- and high-frequency impedance depends on the decoupling stages and the position of the power and ground planes. This paper also shows that achieving practical VPD remains closely tied to challenges such as regulator height, passive-component embedding, and thermal management.

</details>

We first construct a stochastic wrapper policy class parameterized by the LQR gain to enable exploration around a deterministic linear control law. We analyze the additional cost induced by exploration over infinite-horizon, and establish a weak gradient dominance condition that characterizes global optimality under exploration. We then construct a model-free first-order PG algorithm using sampled trajectory information, without identifying the dynamics or approximating a value function. Finally, we establish finite-time convergence guarantees, providing the sufficient number of samples required for near-global optimality with our proposed PG algorithms.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for power-system applications, yet their reliability in engineering analysis remains uncertain. We introduce PS-HalluBench, a manually constructed benchmark of 200 questions that evaluates hallucination-related failures across five power-system task categories: numerical reasoning, network topology, constraint verification, false-premise acceptance, and abstention. We evaluate ten LLMs over the benchmark, yielding 39.35\% overall correctness, with constraint verification achieving only 21.75\%, and highlighting persistent difficulties in engineering reasoning. We further investigate engineering-grounded reasoning through numerical solver support and verified network information both of which show significant performance improvement. The findings reveal important limitations of standalone LLM reasoning and motivate integrating LLMs with verified engineering tools for reliable power-system analysis.

Abstract: Distribution system load restoration is a sequential decision-making problem in which restoration actions must maximize served load, prioritize critical loads, and preserve power-flow feasibility. This paper proposes an explainable agentic artificial intelligence (AI) framework for distribution system load restoration. The proposed framework uses multiple large language model (LLM) agents for planner guidance, strategic plan generation, decision arbitration, memory-based feedback, and explanation generation. Unlike unconstrained LLM-based control, the proposed method restricts agent decisions to feasible restoration actions and evaluates all candidate plans using deterministic AC power-flow feasibility checks. The framework is evaluated on IEEE 33-bus and 69-bus distribution feeders, as well as a larger IEEE 118-bus test case, and compared against reinforcement learning and planning baselines. The results show that the proposed agentic framework achieves better restoration performance while producing structured explanations for operator-facing decision support. A direct comparison with a Single-LLM-Agent baseline further shows that the proposed multi-agent decomposition improves weighted restored load and critical-load recovery compared with a monolithic LLM decision structure.

</details>

Abstract: This paper develops a unified analytical framework for assessing forced oscillations induced by spatially distributed large-load fluctuations to determine their grid-aware fluctuation magnitudes. Emerging computational loads, such as hyperscale datacenters, present cyclic power variations with dynamic impacts depending on the waveform characteristics, forcing frequency, and source location. Our grid-aware framework represents these fluctuations using deterministic harmonic and probabilistic spectral models, and analyzes the resulting bus-wise frequency deviations based on a generic grid frequency-response model. Deterministic, risk-aware, and band-based probabilistic severity measures are further developed to obtain admissible fluctuation limits under different assumptions on frequency and phase uncertainty. We also formulate a security-constrained (SC) optimization of locational fluctuation magnitudes among existing large loads, accounting for their aggregated frequency deviation level. Case studies on the IEEE 39-bus and 14-generator Australian systems validate the proposed severity measures and admissible limit analysis. The SC optimization results further show that distributing a given amount of aggregate fluctuation toward dynamically less sensitive locations can substantially reduce system-wide oscillation severity.

</details>

Abstract: When applied to two-dimensional features such as images, Mamba-based methods either flatten the feature map into 1D sequences and fuse multiple scans, which breaks spatial adjacency and multiplies computation, or recurse directly on the 2D grid but rescale the recurrence with a fixed input-independent factor and aggregate only the predecessors on a single anti-diagonal, leaving the discrete step unconstrained by 2D stability theory. We propose \textbf{FM-Mamba}, a native 2D selective state space model built on the general Fornasini--Marchesini (FM) model. Its coupled recurrence evolves along both spatial axes; cross-axis coupling induces a discrete diagonal-predecessor term $\tilde A_0$, and two independent input-dependent step sizes $Δ_1(i,j)$, $Δ_2(i,j)$ govern propagation along the two axes, so that the coefficients adapt per position and per direction. A normalized ratio $r_k=a_{0,k}/(a_{1,k}a_{2,k})$ yields a closed-form piecewise bound on the two step sizes; when a configuration falls outside the stable interval the steps are projected onto this bound, and the criterion is re-verified per position after training, which suppresses systematic local amplification without global damping. The recurrence is executed by a dual-diagonal wavefront scan: each update gathers information from two anti-diagonals, and the input enters the current state through a single projection, so that neither training nor inference flattens the feature map. Code is available at https://github.com/BitSsm/FM-Mamba-Runtime

</details>

The facet directions are selected from DGR coordinate directions and sensitivity-informed network-critical directions, while the facet locations are determined through a two-step bisection procedure guided by a power-flow feasibility oracle. Numerical results demonstrate that FOPE-sen captures at least 106% more flexibility per dimension than box-shaped and random-direction expansion methods. Moreover, FOPE-sen constructs DOEs comparable to or larger than those of VBPE while using fewer than 1/40 as many facets in dimensions greater than four.
